Many-User Multiple Access with Random User Activity: Achievability Bounds and Efficient Schemes
Xiaoqi Liu, Pablo Pascual Cobo, Ramji Venkataramanan

Abstract
We study the Gaussian multiple access channel with random user activity, in the regime where the number of users is proportional to the code length. The receiver may know some statistics about the number of active users, but does not know the exact number nor the identities of the active users. We derive two achievability bounds on the probabilities of missed detection , false alarm, and active user error, and propose an efficient CDMA-type scheme whose performance can be compared against these bounds. The first bound is a finite-length result based on Gaussian random codebooks and maximum-likelihood decoding. The second is an asymptotic bound, established using spatially coupled Gaussian codebooks and approximate message passing (AMP) decoding. These bounds can be used to compute an achievable tradeoff between the active user density and energy-per-bit, for a fixed user payload and target error rate. The efficient CDMA scheme uses a spatially coupled signature matrix and AMP decoding, and we give rigorous asymptotic guarantees on its error performance. Our analysis provides the first state evolution result for spatially coupled AMP with matrix-valued iterates, which may be of independent interest. Numerical experiments demonstrate the promising error performance of the CDMA scheme for both small and large user payloads, when compared with the two achievability bounds.

1
Introduction

We study the Gaussian multiple access channel (GMAC), with output of the form

\boldsymbol{y}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},

(1)

where
L
is the number of users,
\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
is the codeword of user
\ell
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I})
is random channel noise with spectral density
N_{0}=2\sigma^{2}
, and
n
is the number of channel uses.
Motivated by applications such as the Internet of Things, there has been much interest in the
many-user
regime, where the number of users
L
grows with the code length
n

[
1
,
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
]
.

In this paper, we consider the many-user regime where the number of users
L
grows proportionally with
n
; the ratio
\mu:=L/n
is called the user density
[
3
]
. Each user transmits a fixed number of bits
k
(payload) under a constant energy-per-bit constraint
\|\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\|^{2}_{2}/k\leq E_{b}
. A key question in this regime is to characterize the tradeoff between user density
\mu
, the signal-to-noise ratio
E_{b}/N_{0}
and the error probability in decoding the codewords
\{\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\}
from
\boldsymbol{y}
. The standard error metric is the per-user probability of error

\mathtt{PUPE}:=\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\neq\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}})

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}}
denotes the decoder estimate of
\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}
.
Achievability and converse bounds were derived in
[
3
,
4
,
6
]
in terms of the minimum
E_{b}/N_{0}
needed to achieve a given target
\mathtt{PUPE}
for a given user density
\mu
. Efficient coding schemes were proposed in
[
7
]
in an attempt to approach the converse bound.

In practical GMAC settings, the users are seldom consistently active. Instead, they are active in a sporadic and uncoordinated manner. Motivated by recent work in this direction
[
1
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
, we study the GMAC with
random user activity
.
Letting
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\leq L
denote the (random) number of active users, we consider the proportional regime with
\mathbb{E}[{K_{\mathrm{a}}}]/L\to\alpha

where
\alpha
is a fixed constant. This implies that
\mathbb{E}[{K_{\mathrm{a}}}]/n\to\alpha\mu=:\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
where
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
is also a fixed constant.

The receiver may know the distribution of
K_{\mathrm{a}}
or some statistics, but it does not know the identities of the active users nor the exact value of
K_{\mathrm{a}}
. We study the tradeoff between the
active
user density
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
, the signal-to-noise ratio
E_{b}/N_{0}
and the decoding performance, measured in terms of the probabilities of
missed detection
(
\mathtt{MD}
), false alarm (
\mathtt{FA}
), and active user error (
\mathtt{AUE}
) (see Section
1.3
).

We emphasize that our GMAC setting is different from unsourced random-access
[
3
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
, where all the users share the same codebook, a subset of them are active,
and the decoder recovers the set of transmitted messages (without necessarily recovering the identities of the users who sent the messages).

In contrast, our setting assigns each user a separate codebook,

and the decoder can recover both the transmitted messages and the sender identities.
While unsourced random-access is particularly relevant for grant-free communication systems, coding schemes based on sparse regression for the standard AWGN channel and the GMAC
[
17
]
form the basis of several state-of-the-art schemes for unsourced random-access

[
15
,
16
]
.
Analogously, the coding schemes proposed in this paper can potentially be extended to the unsourced random-access setting.

1.1
Main Contributions

In this work, we establish new information-theoretic bounds and propose an efficient scheme for the many-user GMAC with random user activity.

1.

In Theorem
1
, we derive finite-length achievability bounds on the probabilities of
missed detection
(
\mathtt{MD}
), false alarm (
\mathtt{FA}
), and active user error (
\mathtt{AUE}
)
for an arbitrary distribution of
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
denoted
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
; these error probabilities are defined in Section
1.3
. The bounds are obtained by analyzing random i.i.d. Gaussian codebooks with
joint maximum-likelihood decoding.

2.

Theorem
2
provides an asymptotic achievability bound for the GMAC with random user activity in the regime where
n,L\to\infty
proportionally. The bound is derived by analyzing a scheme with a spatially coupled Gaussian codebook and iterative Approximate Message Passing (AMP) decoding.
For simplicity the bound assumes
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
follows the Binomial distribution
\text{Bin}(\alpha,L)
, but our approach can be generalized to arbitrary
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
.

In the deterministic setting (i.e., when
\alpha=1
),
the bound in Theorem
2
gives a strictly larger achievable region than the bounds by Kowshik in
[
6
]
and Zadik et al.
[
4
]
, the best existing bounds for moderate to large
k
(payload). See Corollary
2
and the discussion below it for details, and Fig.
8
for an example with
k=60
.

The schemes used to derive the bound in Theorem
1
uses joint maximum-likelihood decoding and is infeasible, even for small payloads
k
. The scheme for Theorem
2
uses AMP decoding and can be implemented for small values of
k
, but the codebook size for each user (and hence the decoding complexity) scales exponentially with
k
.

3.

To handle payloads of all sizes,
we propose an efficient CDMA-type scheme, and characterize its asymptotic error performance in Theorem
4
, in terms of
E_{b}/N_{0}
,
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
and
\alpha
.
The scheme has small storage overhead since each user is assigned only a signature sequence rather than an entire codebook.

The scheme uses a spatially coupled Gaussian design matrix (whose columns are the users’ signature sequences) and an AMP decoder tailored to handle random user activity. We propose two different choices for the denoising function used within the AMP decoder: a Bayes-optimal denoiser for small payloads, and a novel thresholding denoiser that is computationally efficient for larger payloads up to hundreds of bits.
Theorem
4
assumes
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}=\text{Bin}(\alpha,L)
for simplicity, but can be extended to general
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
.

The AMP decoder, which aims to recover a matrix-valued signal (with a known prior) from noisy linear observations, can be viewed as a generalization of the spatially coupled AMP algorithm in
[
18
]
for vector-valued signals. To our knowledge, this is the first application of spatial coupling to random linear models with matrix-valued signals, which may be of independent interest.
Theorem
3
provides the asymptotic distributional characterization of the AMP algorithm in this setting.

In Section
4.4
, we compare the two achievability bounds with the efficient CDMA-type scheme. Our numerical results demonstrate the promising performance of the scheme for both small and larger payloads.

We summarize the assumptions and the details of coding schemes used for each of the above results in Table
1
.

Theorem
1

Theorem
2

Theorem
4

Assumptions

Fixed
n,L
,

arbitrary
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}

n,L\to\infty
,
L/n=\mu
,

p_{K_{\mathrm{a}}}=\text{Bin}(\alpha,L)
but can be extended to arbitrary
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}

Codebook

Random codebook

with i.i.d. design

Random codebook

with SC design

CDMA with SC design

Decoding algorithm

Maximum-likelihood

AMP decoding

Memory and computational complexity

Exponential in
k

Linear in
k

Table 1:
Summary of main results, where i.i.d. (SC) design refers to i.i.d. (spatially coupled) Gaussian codebooks, and “Memory and computational complexity” refers to the complexity of the coding scheme
analyzed to obtain the result.

1.2
Related Work

Unsourced random access

Finite-length achievability bounds for unsourced random-access, under various settings, have been established in
[
9
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
]
. The main results in
[
9
,
10
]
are stated in terms of the joint-user error probability, with
[
10
]
also providing bounds on the
\mathtt{PUPE}
. The probabilities of
\mathtt{MD}
and
\mathtt{FA}
are quantified separately in
[
11
,
12
]
and
[
13
]
. Our finite length achievability bounds in Theorem

1
are similar to those in
[
11
]
, but a key difference is that in our setting the users have distinct codebooks, and we derive bounds on the probabilities of
\mathtt{MD},\mathtt{FA}
, and
\mathtt{AUE}
separately.

Approximate Message Passing

AMP is a class of first-order iterative algorithms initially proposed for estimation in random linear models
[
19
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
. It has since been applied to a variety of high-dimensional estimation problems such as low-rank matrix estimation
[
23
,
24
,
25
]
and generalized linear models
[
26
,
27
,
28
]
. Two appealing features of AMP are: i) it can be tailored to take advantage of the signal prior, and ii) under suitable model assumptions, its estimation performance in the high-dimensional limit can be characterized by a simple deterministic recursion called state evolution. For a variety of high-dimensional statistical estimation problems, AMP has been shown to be optimal among first-order methods
[
29
]
, and is conjectured to achieve the optimal asymptotic estimation error among polynomial-time algorithms
[
30
]
. We refer the reader to
[
31
]
for a survey on AMP algorithms for various statistical models.

Spatial coupling

Coding schemes based on spatially coupled random linear models with AMP decoding have been shown to be capacity-achieving for point-to-point channels
[
32
,
33
,
34
]
, and give the best achievable tradeoffs for the many-user GMAC
[
7
,
6
,
35
]
. These works show that spatially coupled designs significantly improve on the performance of i.i.d. designs in certain regimes.
More generally, spatially coupled designs with AMP decoding have been shown to achieve the Bayes-optimal estimation error for both linear models
[
18
]
and generalized linear models
[
36
]
(with vector-valued signals). To our knowledge, Theorem
3
presents the first state evolution result for AMP applied to spatially coupled linear models with a matrix-valued signal.

CDMA-based schemes

The asymptotic error performance of CDMA with i.i.d. signature sequences
has been studied in a number of works in the absence of random user activity

[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
. Assuming the signature sequences are i.i.d. sub-Gaussian, AMP is the best known decoding algorithm
[
20
,
21
]
. Recently, variants of CDMA have been studied for activity detection in multiple-antenna networks
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
. The decoding task in these settings is an instance of the multiple measurement vector (MMV) problem, where the goal is to recover a matrix signal (with a specific prior) from noisy linear observations. The AMP algorithm for the MMV problem

[
45
]
is used for decoding in
[
42
,
43
]
. However, the design matrix in all these works is i.i.d., whereas we use a spatially coupled design, which requires a novel AMP algorithm.

1.3
Performance Metrics

Let
\mathcal{C^{(\ell)}}=\{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}^{(\ell)},\boldsymbol{c}_{2}^{(\ell)},\dots,\boldsymbol{c}_{M}^{(\ell)}\}
denote the codebook of user
\ell
. Each active user transmits
k
bits, so
M=2^{k}
. Let
w_{\ell}\in\{\emptyset,1,2,\dots,M\}
denote the index of the codeword chosen by user
\ell
, where
w_{\ell}=\emptyset
indicates that the user is silent (not active).
We use index pair
(i,j)
to refer to
the
j
th codeword of the
i
th codebook.
Then the set of transmitted codewords can be defined as
\mathcal{W}:=\{(\ell,w_{\ell}):w_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}
, and the GMAC channel output can be written as

\boldsymbol{y}=\sum_{\ell:(\ell,w_{\ell})\in\mathcal{W}}\boldsymbol{c}_{w_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon},

(2)

where the number of active users is
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}=\left\lvert\mathcal{W}\right\rvert\leq L
.

Given the channel output
\boldsymbol{y}
and the codebooks
\{\mathcal{C}^{(\ell)}\}
, the decoder aims to recover the set of transmitted codewords.
Let
\widehat{w}_{\ell}\in\{\emptyset,1,2,\dots,M\}
denote the decoded codeword in the
\ell
th codebook, and let
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}:=\{(\ell,\widehat{w}_{\ell}):\widehat{w}_{\ell}\neq\emptyset\}
denote the set of decoded codewords, or the decoded set in short, with size
|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}|=:{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}
.

Such a decoder can make three types of errors, which we call
missed detection
(
\mathtt{MD}
), false alarm (
\mathtt{FA}
) and active user error (
\mathtt{AUE}
).
\mathtt{MD}
refer to an active user declared silent, and
\mathtt{FA}
to a silent user declared active. An
\mathtt{AUE}
occurs when an active users is correctly declared active, but the decoded codeword is incorrect. Given the codebooks
\mathcal{C}^{(1)},\dots,\mathcal{C}^{(L)}
, the error probabilities corresponding to these events are defined as follows:

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{MD}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell:(\ell,w_{\ell})\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}\{\widehat{w_{\ell}}=\emptyset\}\right],

(3)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{FA}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}\sum_{\ell:(\ell,\widehat{w}_{\ell})\in\widehat{\mathcal{W}}}\mathbbm{1}\{w_{\ell}=\emptyset\}\right],

(4)

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{AUE}}

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell:(\ell,w_{\ell})\in\mathcal{W}}\mathbbm{1}\big\{\widehat{w_{\ell}}\notin\{w_{\ell},\emptyset\}\big\}\right].

(5)

Here, the expectation is taken over
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
,
{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}
, and the uniform distribution over messages for each active user.

Notation

The indicator function of an event
\mathcal{A}
is denoted by
\mathbbm{1}\{\mathcal{A}\}
. For positive integers
a
and
b
,
[a]:=\{1,...,a\}
and
[a:b]=\{a,a+1,\dots,b\}
. We use boldface letters for vectors and matrices and plain font for scalars. We use
A_{ij}
to denote the
(i,j)
-th entry of matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
.
Let
x^{+}=\max\{x,0\}
.
We use i.i.d. as shorthand for independently and identically distributed.

We write
\mathcal{N}_{d}(\boldsymbol{\mu},\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
for a
d
-dimensional Gaussian with mean
\boldsymbol{\mu}
and covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
.
We use
\text{Bin}(p,n)
to denote a Binomial distribution with
n
trials and success probability
p
.
We write
\text{Gamma}(k,\theta)
for the Gamma distribution with shape
k
and scale
\theta
. We denote the Gamma function by
\Gamma(k)=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{k-1}e^{-t}\textrm{d}t
, and the lower and upper incomplete Gamma functions by
\gamma(k,w)=\int_{0}^{w}t^{k-1}e^{-t}\textrm{d}t
and
\Gamma(k,w)=\int_{w}^{\infty}t^{k-1}e^{-t}\textrm{d}t
.

2
Finite-
L
ength Random Coding Achievability Bounds

In this section, we derive finite-length achievability bounds on
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
for
a given
E_{b}/N_{0}
,
L
and
n
, using a random coding scheme with maximum-likelihood decoding. Without loss of generality, we take
\sigma^{2}=N_{0}/2=1
, so the constraint on
E_{b}/N_{0}
is enforced via
E_{b}
.
The number of active users
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
is
assumed to be drawn from a distribution
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
.

2.1
Random Coding and Maximum-
L
ikelihood Decoding

Recall that each active user transmits
k
bits using a length
n
codeword. For
\ell\in[L]
,
we construct the codebook
\mathcal{C}^{(\ell)}
with codewords

\boldsymbol{c}_{j}^{(\ell)}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}^{(\ell)}\mathbbm{1}\{\|\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}^{(\ell)}\|_{2}^{2}\leq E_{b}k\}
, where

\tilde{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}^{(\ell)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},E_{b}^{\prime}k/n\boldsymbol{I})
and
0<E_{b}^{\prime}<E_{b}
. Note that the codewords across codebooks are distinct almost surely. The indicator function ensures the codewords
\boldsymbol{c}_{j}^{(\ell)}
satisfy the energy-per-bit constraint
\|\boldsymbol{c}_{j}^{(\ell)}\|_{2}^{2}/k\leq E_{b}
. Recall that the transmitted codeword of user
\ell\in[L]
is denoted by
\boldsymbol{c}_{w_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}
. The decoder
first computes the maximum-likelihood estimate
{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
of the number of active users
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
, via

{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\argmax_{K\in[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]}\,p(\boldsymbol{y}|{K_{\mathrm{a}}}=K)

(6)

where
{\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
are deterministic integers between 0 and
L
, chosen such that
\mathbb{P}({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}])
is small (e.g.,
10^{-6}
). This step is similar to the scheme in
[
11
]
.
Using
{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
, the decoder then produces an estimate
\{\boldsymbol{c}_{\widehat{w}_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\,,\,\ell\in[L]\}
of the transmitted set of codewords
\{\boldsymbol{c}_{w_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\,,\,\ell\in[L]\}
by solving a combinatorial least squares problem.
Let
w_{\ell}^{\prime}\in\{\emptyset,1,2,\dots,M\}
for
\ell\in[L]
and define
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}:=\{(\ell,w^{\prime}_{\ell}):w_{\ell}^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\}
. The decoder computes:

\{\widehat{w_{1}},\widehat{w_{2}},\dots,\widehat{w_{L}}\}=\argmin_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{w_{1}^{\prime},w_{2}^{\prime},\dots,w_{L}^{\prime}\}:\\
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}\leq\left\lvert\mathcal{W}^{\prime}\right\rvert\leq\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}\end{subarray}}\|c(\mathcal{W}^{\prime})-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}^{2}\,,\quad\text{where}\quad c(\mathcal{W}^{\prime}):=\sum_{\ell:(\ell,w_{\ell}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{c}_{w_{\ell}^{\prime}}^{(\ell)}\,.

(7)

Here,
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\max\{{\kappa_{l}},{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{r_{l}}\}
and
\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\min\{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}},{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}+{r_{\mathrm{u}}}\}
, where
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}\geq 0
are prespecified
lower and upper
decoding
radii like in
[
11
]
.
In words, the decoder
searches over every set
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
with size between
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
and
\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
, which contains at most one codeword from each codebook, to minimize the distance between the sum of the codewords in
\mathcal{W}^{\prime}
and the channel output
\boldsymbol{y}
.
Setting
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}
provides equal control over
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
, whereas setting
{r_{l}}<{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
controls
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
more strictly than
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
, and vice versa (see Fig.
1c
).

Choosing
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
gives the biggest search range with
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}
and
\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
.
The complexity of this decoder grows exponentially with
n
, making it computationally infeasible.

Applying this decoder,
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
averaged over the ensemble of joint-codebooks
(\mathcal{C}^{(\ell)})_{\ell\in[L]}
described above can
individually
satisfy certain upper bounds
p_{\mathrm{MD}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
, respectively. However, time-sharing among two deterministic joint-codebooks from this ensemble is required to guarantee that all three bounds are achieved
simultaneously
, see
[
10
, Theorem 8]
and
[
11
, Remark 2]
. Time-sharing is a common randomization technique over deterministic coding schemes to achieve performance that no single deterministic scheme can attain

[
46
,
10
]
. With time-sharing,
an error analysis of the random coding scheme with maximum-likelihood decoding leads to the following result.

Theorem 1

(Finite-length achievability bounds)
.

Let
0<E^{\prime}_{b}<E_{b}
and
P=E_{b}k/n
,
P^{\prime}=E_{b}^{\prime}k/n
. The decoding
radii
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}\geq 0
,
and
{\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
satisfying
0\leq{\kappa_{l}}\leq{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\leq L
are defined as above.

There exists a randomized coding scheme (with time-sharing)
using the decoder described in (
6
)–(
7
) that achieves
error probabilities satisfying

p_{\mathrm{MD}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}
,
p_{\mathrm{FA}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}

simultaneously
.
The quantities
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
are defined as follows in terms of the distribution of the number of active users
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
:

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}=\tilde{p}\,+\,\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\sum_{{\hat{t}}\in{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}}}\min\{p(t,{\hat{t}}),\xi({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})\}\cdot\sum_{\psi=0}^{{\psi_{\mathrm{u}}}}\frac{\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)}{\nu({t_{\min}})}\Delta_{\mathrm{MD}}\,,

(8)

\displaystyle\qquad\Delta_{\mathrm{MD}}=\frac{({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}+(t-{\hat{t}})^{+}+\psi}{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}}\,,\quad\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\min\{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}+{r_{\mathrm{u}}}\},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}=\tilde{p}\,+\,\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\sum_{{\hat{t}}\in{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}}}\min\{p(t,{\hat{t}}),\xi({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})\}\cdot\sum_{\psi=0}^{{\psi_{\mathrm{u}}}}\frac{\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)}{\nu({t_{\min}})}\Delta_{\mathrm{FA}}\,,

(9)

\displaystyle\qquad\Delta_{\mathrm{FA}}=\frac{(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}+({\hat{t}}-t)^{+}+\psi}{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-t-({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}+{\hat{t}}+(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}}\,,\quad\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\max\{{\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{r_{l}}\},

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}=\tilde{p}\,+\,\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}\sum_{t\in{\mathcal{T}}}\sum_{{\hat{t}}\in{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}}}\min\{p(t,{\hat{t}}),\xi({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})\}\cdot\sum_{\psi=0}^{{\psi_{\mathrm{u}}}}\frac{\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)}{\nu({t_{\min}})}\frac{{t_{\min}}-\psi}{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}}\,.

(10)

Here,

\displaystyle\tilde{p}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\big({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]\big)+\mathbb{E}[{K_{\mathrm{a}}}]\frac{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2},\frac{nP}{2P^{\prime}})}{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2})}\,,

(11)

and the sets
{\mathcal{T}},{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}}
are defined as:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}}=\Big[0:\min\{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}\}\Big]\,,\quad{\widehat{\mathcal{T}}_{t}}=\Big[\left\{t+({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}-({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}\right\}^{+}:{t_{\mathrm{u}}}\Big]\,,

(12)

\displaystyle\quad\text{ where }\ {t_{\mathrm{u}}}=\min\left\{\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+},\;t+(\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}-(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}\right\}\,.

(13)

The remaining quantities in the definitions of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
are defined as:

\displaystyle p(t,{\hat{t}})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{n}{2}E(t,{\hat{t}})\right)\,,\quad E(t,{\hat{t}})=\max_{\rho,\rho_{1}\in[0,1]}\big[-\rho\rho_{1}R_{1}(t,{\hat{t}})-\rho_{1}R_{2}(t)+E_{0}(\rho,\rho_{1})\big]\,,

(14)

\displaystyle\quad\;\text{where}\quad R_{1}(t,{\hat{t}})=\frac{2}{n}\left({t_{\min}}\ln M+\ln\binom{\mathcal{R}}{{t_{\min}}}\right)\,,\qquad

(15)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\quad\;\;\,{t_{\min}}=\min\{t,{\hat{t}}\}\,,\quad\mathcal{R}=L-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}+{t_{\min}}-(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}-({\hat{t}}-t)^{+}\geq{t_{\min}}\,,

(16)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\qquad\;\;R_{2}(t)=\frac{2}{n}\ln\binom{\min\{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}\}}{t}\,,

(17)

\displaystyle\quad\;\qquad\quad\;E_{0}(\rho,\rho_{1})=\max_{\lambda>-\frac{1}{P^{\prime}\hat{t}}}\Big[\rho_{1}a(\rho,\lambda)+\ln\big(1-\rho_{1}P_{1}b(\rho,\lambda)\big)\Big]\,,

(18)

\displaystyle\quad\;\qquad\quad\;a(\rho,\lambda)=\rho\ln\big(1+P^{\prime}{\hat{t}}\lambda\big)+\ln\big(1+P^{\prime}t\chi(\rho,\lambda)\big)\,,

(19)

\displaystyle\quad\;\qquad\quad\;b(\rho,\lambda)=\rho\lambda-\frac{\chi(\rho,\lambda)}{1+P^{\prime}t\chi(\rho,\lambda)}\,,\qquad\chi(\rho,\lambda)=\frac{\rho\lambda}{1+P^{\prime}{\hat{t}}\lambda}\,,

(20)

\displaystyle\quad\;\qquad\quad\;P_{1}=\left(({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}+(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}\right)P^{\prime}+1\,,

(21)

\displaystyle\xi({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\min_{\kappa\in[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]\setminus{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}}\left\{\mathbbm{1}\{\kappa<{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}\}\frac{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2},\zeta)}{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2})}+\mathbbm{1}\{\kappa>{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}\}\frac{\gamma(\frac{n}{2},\zeta)}{\Gamma(\frac{n}{2})}\right\}\,,

(22)

\displaystyle\quad\;\text{where}\quad\zeta=\frac{n}{2(1+{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}P^{\prime})}\ln\left(\frac{1+\kappa P^{\prime}}{1+{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}P^{\prime}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{1+{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}P^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{1+\kappa P^{\prime}}\right]^{-1}\,,

(23)

\displaystyle\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)=\binom{\mathcal{R}-{t_{\min}}}{\psi}M^{\psi}\cdot\binom{{t_{\min}}}{{t_{\min}}-\psi}(M-1)^{{t_{\min}}-\psi},\quad\psi\in[0:{\psi_{\mathrm{u}}}]\,,

(24)

\displaystyle\nu({t_{\min}})=\sum_{\psi=0}^{{\psi_{\mathrm{u}}}}\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)\,,\quad\;{\psi_{\mathrm{u}}}=\min\left\{{t_{\min}},\mathcal{R}-{t_{\min}}\right\}\,.

(25)

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of the bounds for unsourced random access in
[
11
, Theorem 1]
, involving union bounds over error events via a change of measure and applications of Chernoff bound and Gallager’s
\rho
-trick.
The proof is detailed in Section
5
, where
we present the preliminaries for the proof in Section
5.1
, followed by the proof for a simple special case in Section
5.2
, and the general case in Section
5.3
.

The key additional technical step in our setting is the careful separation of error events into the three categories defined in (
3
),(
4
), and (
5
), rather than only considering
\mathtt{MD}
s and
\mathtt{FA}
s as in
[
11
]
. This separation of error events is illustrated in Figs.
13
and
14
and mathematically formalized in Lemmas
7
and
9
. Since users have distinct codebooks in our setting, the number of codewords in each category is different from
[
11
]
(see e.g., Remark
2
), leading to different expressions for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
.

∎

Remarks

1.

Theorem
1
reduces to the achievability bound in
[
3
, Section IV]
by Polyanskiy when all users are active and the number of users is known, i.e.,
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}={K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=L
with probability 1 and the decoding
radii are
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=0
. In this case, all errors are
\mathtt{AUE}
s.

2.

For the tightest possible achievability bounds,
P^{\prime}
should be optimized over the interval
(0,P)
using methods such as the golden section search
[
47
,
48
]
. To reduce the computational cost of the bounds,
we may choose a fixed value such as
P^{\prime}=0.8P

instead. Fig.
3

and surrounding text
in Section
2.3
demonstrates how the choice of
P^{\prime}
might affect the bounds.

3.

Given
\rho,\rho_{1},t,{\hat{t}}
, the optimal
\lambda
in (
18
) admits a closed-form solution, same as the one given in Remark vii) following Theorem 1 in
[
11
]
.

4.

An alternative achievability bound may be derived using information densities, analogous to
[
3
,
11
]
. However, such bounds are very expensive to evaluate numerically and are expected to be largely dominated by the error-exponent-type bounds in Theorem
1
.

2.2
Error Floors

As before, we take
N_{0}=2\sigma^{2}=2
and control the signal-to-noise ratio
E_{b}/N_{0}=nP/(2k)
through
P
. The bounds for
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in (
9
), (
8
), (
10
) are each the sum of
\tilde{p}
and another term.
Corollary
1
below highlights a key observation: the second term in
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
does not vanish when
P\to\infty
, unless we choose large enough decoding
radii
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
that grow with the problem size, e.g.,
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
. In contrast, the second term
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
vanishes as
P\to\infty
. We give a heuristic discussion of these error floors before stating the result.

We note that
\tilde{p}
in (
11
) reduces to
\mathbb{P}\big({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]\big)
as
P\to\infty
, and that the optimal value of
P^{\prime}
that minimizes the error probabilities should grow with
P
, i.e.,
P^{\prime}\to\infty
.
As
P^{\prime}\to\infty
, our maximum-likelihood decoder in (
7
) is guaranteed to decode the transmitted set of codewords correctly, provided the number of active users
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
falls within the decoder’s search range
[\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}:\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}]
, where we recall
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\max\{{\kappa_{l}},{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{r_{l}}\}
and
\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}=\min\{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}},{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}+{r_{\mathrm{u}}}\}
.
The term
\tilde{p}
in the error bounds accounts for the bad event where
\left\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]\right\}
. Conditioned on the good event that
\left\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\in[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]\right\}
, we can ensure that
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\in[\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}:\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}]
by selecting large enough decoding
radii
, e.g.,
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
, such that
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}
and
\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
. In this case, the error probability bounds in Theorem
1
reduce to
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}=\tilde{p}
.

However, choosing such large decoding
radii
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
make the bounds infeasible to compute for even moderately large
L
. With fixed
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
that do not scale with
L
, the search range
[\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}:\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}]
may not contain
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
, and therefore as
P\to\infty
,

•

if
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}<\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
, the decoder commits at least
(\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{K_{\mathrm{a}}})

\mathtt{FA}
s;

•

if
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}>\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
, the decoder commits at least
({K_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})

\mathtt{MD}
s.

This implies that the bounds
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
in Theorem
1
exhibit error floors. In contrast,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
vanishes as
P\to\infty
.
This limiting behaviour of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
matches that found in
[
11
, Corollary 1]
, and we recall that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
does not feature in the unsourced set-up in
[
11
]
.
These results are summarized below.

Corollary 1

(Error floors)
.

Let
\bar{p}=\mathbb{P}({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}])
, then the error bounds
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in (
8
)–(
10
) satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to\infty}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}}=\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}\frac{({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}}{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}}\xi({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})+\bar{p}\ ,

(26)

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to\infty}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}=\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})\sum_{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}}^{{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}}\frac{(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}}{{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})^{+}+(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}}\xi({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})+\bar{p}\ ,

(27)

\displaystyle\lim_{P\to\infty}\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{AUE}}=\bar{p}\ .

(28)

Note that
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}}=\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}=\bar{p}
in (
26
) and (
27
) when
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
are chosen to be sufficiently large such that
\underline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{l}}
and
\overline{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the proof of
[
11
, Corollary 1]
and is provided in Section
5.4
.
∎

2.3
Numerical Results

(a)
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=0
, 1 or 2.

(b)
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=0
or
L
.

(c)
{r_{l}}=0
,
{r_{\mathrm{u}}}=2
, or
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
.

Figure 1:
Three types of errors
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in Theorem
1
(thicker curves) and the error floors
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}},\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in Corollary
1
(thinner horizontal lines) plotted against
E_{b}/N_{0}
, with different choices of decoding
radii
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
. Different colours correspond to different types of errors; different line styles correspond to different
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
. Shaded regions: larger
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
cause higher errors due to noise overfitting.
n=2000,L=50,k=8
,
\alpha=0.5
,
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}=\text{Bin}(\alpha,L),

P^{\prime}
is optimized over
(0,P)
, and
({\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})
are chosen so that
\mathbb{P}({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}])\leq 10^{-13}
.

We numerically evaluate the error bounds
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in Theorem
1
, and the error floors
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}},\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{AUE}}
of these error bounds in Corollary
1
as
E_{b}/N_{0}\to\infty
.
While Theorem
1
and Corollary
1
hold for any arbitrary probability distribution
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
,
we assume
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}
to be the Binomial distribution

\text{Bin}(\alpha,L)
in our numerical experiments throughout this section.

We use
\max\{p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
as
an overall error metric
,
since
\mathtt{MD}
s and
\mathtt{FA}
s largely exhibit a one-to-one correspondence. (This is easily seen in
the special case where
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}={\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}
, since each active user mistakenly declared silent corresponds to a silent user mistakenly declared active, creating an exact one-to-one correspondence between the
\mathtt{MD}
s and
\mathtt{FA}
s.)
Our implementation of the bounds in Theorem
1
and Corollary
1
is adapted from the MATLAB code provided in
[
11
]

and is available at
[
49
]
.

Fig.
1
plots the error bounds
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
as thick curves and the error floors
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}},\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{AUE}}
as thin horizontal lines. We observe that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
decrease with
E_{b}/N_{0}
, and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
are lower bounded by and converge to the error floors
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}
as
E_{b}/N_{0}
increases. The error floor of
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
is
\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{AUE}}=10^{-13}
, which is omitted from Fig.
1a
.
The different line styles correspond to different choices of decoding
radii
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
.
Recall that larger
{r_{l}}
and
{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
make the maximum-likelihood decoder in (
7
) search over a larger set of values for
{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}
.
We observe that while larger
{r_{l}}
and
{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
yield fewer errors at higher
E_{b}/N_{0}
, it can result in higher errors at lower
E_{b}/N_{0}
due to
noise overfitting
(see shaded regions in the subfigures),
where the decoder’s larger search space increases the likelihood of mistakenly fitting to the channel noise realization rather than to the signal.
This phenomenon was also highlighted in
[
11
]
.

Fig.
1b
compares the errors when
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=0
or
L
, where
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
makes the decoder search for
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
through all sets of codewords with size
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\in[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}]
. Note that with
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
,
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
all converge to the error floor
10^{-13}
for large
E_{b}/N_{0}
, as expected due to Corollary
1
.
Fig.
1c
presents the asymmetric case where
{r_{l}}=0
and
{r_{\mathrm{u}}}=2
. This biases the decoder toward declaring users active rather than silent, resulting in lower
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
than
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
. Analogously, one can control
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
more strictly than
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
by choosing
{r_{l}}>{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
.

In Fig.
12
in Section
4.4
, we plot the bound as the minimum
E_{b}/N_{0}
required to achieve a target total error of
\max\{p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+p_{\mathrm{AUE}}\leq 0.01
for a range of active user densities
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
.

The error floors in Corollary
1
are useful in informing our choice of parameters such as
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
,
{\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
and
P^{\prime}
. This is explored in Figs.
2
–
3
, which present the contour plots of the total error floor
\max\{\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{MD}},\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{AUE}}
for varying active user density
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}=\alpha L/n
(
x
-axis), and different choices of decoding
radii
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=r
normalized by the standard deviation of
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}
(
y
-axis).
The contour plots give insight into how the performance of the maximum-likelihood decoder depends on
{r_{l}},{r_{\mathrm{u}}},{\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
and
P^{\prime}
.
We discuss this below, noting that the shape of the plots may also depend on the (possibly suboptimal) bounding techniques in our analysis.

In
Fig.
2
,

{\kappa_{l}}
and
{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}
are chosen to be the largest and smallest integers, respectively, so that
\mathbb{P}({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}])\leq\bar{p}
.
Fig.
2
illustrates the effect of different choices of
\bar{p}
. For smaller values of
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
, e.g.,
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}=0.112
(marked by the left gray vertical line in each subfigure), a smaller
\bar{p}
thus a larger search range can achieve a smaller error floor. Indeed, observe that while choosing
\bar{p}=10^{-3}
yields an error floor as low as
0.02
, reducing
\bar{p}
to
10^{-7}
further lowers the error floor to
0.01
.
In contrast, larger values of
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
correspond to a more challenging decoding task, where the decoder observes increased interference among active users and attempts to recover a higher user payload. In such cases, choosing a larger
\bar{p}
prevents the maximum-likelihood decoder from overfitting to noise. Indeed, at
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}=0.319
for instance (marked by the right gray vertical line in each subfigure), choosing
\bar{p}=10^{-3}
instead of
\bar{p}=10^{-7}
leads to a significantly lower error floor.

(a)
\bar{p}=10^{-3}

(b)
\bar{p}=10^{-7}

r/\mathrm{std}({K_{\mathrm{a}}})
(

.

, where

,

r=L
.

Contour plots of

\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
(

-axis) and normalized decoding radius
r/\mathrm{std}({K_{\mathrm{a}}})
(

-axis)
where
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=r
. Subfigures use different
P^{\prime}
, indicated below the subfigures.
L=600,k=6,\alpha=0.7
,

,

corresponds to
