, and
({\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})
are chosen so that
\mathbb{P}({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}])\leq 10^{-4}
.

Fig.
3
compares different choices of
P^{\prime}\in(0,P)
with
\bar{p}
fixed to be
10^{-4}
. We observe that the error floor can be significantly lowered by choosing
P^{\prime}
not too close to
P
, especially for larger values of
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
. Nevertheless, our experiments indicate that the optimal value of
P^{\prime}
varies on a case-by-case basis; it tends to be closer to
P
for larger
L
and
n
. Hence, whenever computational resources permit, such as in Fig.
1
, we optimize
P^{\prime}
over
(0,P)
using the golden section search method.

Computational cost

A shortcoming of the finite-length bounds in Theorem
1
is their high computational complexity, which is
\mathcal{O}(L^{5})
due to the five summations in each of the equations (
8
)–(
10
). This limits their applicability to relatively small values of
L
and
n
. In the next section (Section
3
), we derive an asymptotic bound which is significantly cheaper to evaluate, with computational complexity independent of
L
or
n
.

3
Asymptotic Random Coding Achievability Bounds

In this section, we provide two asymptotic bounds on
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in the limit of
n,L\to\infty
with
\alpha=\mathbb{E}[{K_{\mathrm{a}}}]/L
and
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}=\mathbb{E}[{K_{\mathrm{a}}}]/n
held constant. The first bound is tighter but hard to compute for large payloads, whereas the second is looser but can be computed efficiently for large payloads.

3.1
Random Coding

We consider a random coding scheme similar to that in Section
2.1
under AMP decoding instead of maximum-likelihood decoding. As before, the codebook of each user
\ell\in[L]
is populated with
M=2^{k}
random codewords that satisfy the energy-per-bit constraint. When active, each user selects a codeword from their codebook uniformly at random for transmission. For simplicity, we consider the prior where each user
\ell
is independently active with probability
\alpha
, i.e.,
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}=\text{Bin}(\alpha,L)
, but the bounds can be adapted to other priors as well.

The random coding scheme can be formulated in terms a linear model. Let
\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times M}
be the matrix whose columns store the codewords of user
\ell
, scaled by
{1}/{\sqrt{E_{b}k}}
.
This scaling ensures the columns of
\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}
have unit squared
\ell_{2}
-norm in expectation, which is required by the standard AMP framework. Therefore the codeword transmitted by user
\ell
can be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\,,

(29)

where
\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}
with

\displaystyle p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}=(1-\alpha)\delta_{\boldsymbol{0}}+\alpha p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}}\,,\quad p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\delta_{\sqrt{E}\boldsymbol{e}_{j}}\,,\quad E=E_{b}k.

(30)

Here,
\delta_{\boldsymbol{0}}
is the unit mass at

\boldsymbol{0}
, and
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
denotes the canonical basis vector with one in the
j
th coordinate and zeros everywhere else. This implies that

\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}

is all-zero when the user is silent, or one-sparse when the user is active, with the nonzero coordinate uniformly distributed in
\{1,\ldots,M\}
. The vector
\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}
can be interpreted as the message vector of user
\ell
.

Figure 4:
Coding scheme for the proof of asymptotic achievability bounds. The joint message vector
\boldsymbol{x}=[\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{\top},\dots,\boldsymbol{x}_{L}^{\top}]^{\top}
has
L
sections corresponding to the
L
users. Each section is drawn i.i.d. from
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}
. The joint codebook matrix
\boldsymbol{A}=[\boldsymbol{A}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{A}_{L}]
has
L
sections, with the
\ell
-th section storing the codewords of the
\ell
-th user as columns of the matrix.

Let
\boldsymbol{A}=[\boldsymbol{A}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{A}_{L}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times LM}
and
\boldsymbol{x}=[\boldsymbol{x}_{1}^{\top},\dots,\boldsymbol{x}_{L}^{\top}]^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{LM}
be the concatenation of the random matrices and the message vectors of all users, respectively. Then the channel output from the GMAC in (
1
) takes the form

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(31)

where
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}_{n}(\boldsymbol{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I})
is the channel noise vector. See Fig.
4
for an illustration.

The decoding task is to recover the concatenated message vector
\boldsymbol{x}
from
\boldsymbol{y}
and
\boldsymbol{A}
. This problem is similar to the decoding of Sparse Regression Codes (SPARCs)
[
50
,
32
,
17
,
34
]
, the key difference being that in SPARCs, the prior
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}
does not contain a mass at
\boldsymbol{0}
. Based on the similarity with SPARCs, we use a scheme with a spatially coupled matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
and Approximate Message Passing (AMP) decoding to obtain asymptotic bounds on the three probabilities of error. Achievable regions for the many-user GMAC (without random access) were obtained using similar ideas in
[
7
]
and
[
6
]
.

Spatially coupled design

Figure 5:
A spatially coupled design matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
constructed using a base matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
according to (
32
). The base matrix shown here is an
(\omega,\Lambda)
base matrix (defined in Definition
1
) with parameters
\omega=3,\Lambda=7
. The white parts of
\boldsymbol{A}
and
\boldsymbol{W}
correspond to zeros.

Inspired by
[
34
,
7
,
6
]
, we choose
\boldsymbol{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times LM}
to be a spatially coupled random Gaussian matrix to ensure the tightest achievability bounds under AMP decoding. Fig.
5
illustrates an example of such a matrix.
The matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
is divided into
R

row blocks
and
C

column blocks
, where
R
is chosen to divide
n
and
C
to divide
L
. This implies that every column block contains
\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}
sections of
\boldsymbol{A}
, corresponding to
\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}
users. Let
\textsf{r}(\cdot):[n]\to[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}(\cdot):[LM]\to[\textsf{C}]
be the operators that map a particular row or column index into its corresponding row block or column block index. Then for
i\in[n]
and
j\in[LM]
, the entries of
\boldsymbol{A}
are drawn according to

\displaystyle A_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}\left(0,\frac{1}{n/\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}(j)}\right),\quad\text{for}\;i\in[n],\ j\in[LM].

(32)

Here,
\boldsymbol{W}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\textsf{R}\times\textsf{C}}
is a
base matrix
, whose columns satisfy
\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}=1
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
. This implies that the columns of
\boldsymbol{A}
have unit squared
\ell_{2}
-norm in expectation. By concentration of measure, it follows that
\|\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}/k=\|\boldsymbol{A}_{\ell}\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}/k\to E_{b}
as
n\to\infty
, indicating that the energy-per-bit constraint is asymptotically satisfied. The base matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
we use to obtain our bounds is defined in Definition
1
below through two parameters
\omega
and
\Lambda
.
The standard i.i.d. Gaussian design where
A_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1/n)
is a special case of the spatially coupled design, obtained by using a base matrix with a single entry
W=1
(
\textsf{R}=\textsf{C}=1
).

Definition 1
.

An
(\omega,\Lambda)
base matrix
\boldsymbol{W}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\textsf{R}\times\textsf{C}}
is described by two parameters: the coupling width
\omega\geq 1
and the coupling length
\Lambda\geq 2\omega-1
. The matrix has
\textsf{R}=\Lambda+\omega-1
rows and
\textsf{C}=\Lambda
columns, with each column having
\omega
identical nonzero entries in the band-diagonal and zeros everywhere else. For
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, the
(\textsf{r},\textsf{c})
th entry of the base matrix is given by

W_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}=\begin{cases}\ \frac{1}{\omega}\quad&\text{if}\ \textsf{c}\leq\textsf{r}\leq\textsf{c}+\omega-1,\\
\ 0\quad&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(33)

Each nonzero block of the spatially coupled matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
can be viewed as an (uncoupled) i.i.d. matrix with
\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}
users and
\frac{n}{\textsf{R}}
channel uses. Hence we define the
inner
user density to be

\displaystyle\mu_{\text{in}}=\frac{L/\textsf{C}}{n/\textsf{R}}=\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\mu=\left(1+\frac{\omega-1}{\Lambda}\right)\mu.

(34)

Note that since
\omega\geq 1
, we have
\mu_{\text{in}}>\mu
. The difference between
\mu_{\text{in}}
and
\mu
becomes negligible when
\Lambda\gg\omega
. This relation will be used later in the asymptotic performance analysis of AMP decoding with the spatially coupled design matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
.

The benefit of using a spatially coupled design compared to a standard i.i.d. matrix is due to its band-diagonal structure, as shown in Fig.
5
. Each row in
\boldsymbol{A}
corresponds to a channel transmission, while each group of
M
columns corresponds to the codebook of a user. (Columns
1
to
M
correspond to user 1, columns
(M+1)
to
2M
correspond to user 2, and so on.) For illustration, suppose that each of the blocks in
\boldsymbol{A}
in Fig.
5
corresponds to 5 users and 5 transmissions. As there is a single non-zero block in the top row block of
\boldsymbol{A}
, the first 5 transmissions (corresponding to the first row block) will only involve interference from the first 5 users and therefore the first 5 messages are easier to decode. As the next row-block contains two non-zero blocks, the next 5 transmissions will involve 10 users, but if the first 5 users’ messages have been decoded, the next 5 messages will also be easier to decode. This generates a
snowball
effect, where as the iterations progress, the adjacent users’ messages present less interference. Gradually the full set of messages can be decoded, resulting in a gain in performance compared to the standard i.i.d. Gaussian scheme. An illustration of the decoding progression can be found in
[
51
, Fig. 2.2]
.

3.2
AMP Decoding and State Evolution

The channel decoding task is to estimate the joint message vector
\boldsymbol{x}
given the channel output
\boldsymbol{y}
, the design matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
and the channel noise variance
\sigma^{2}
. We use an AMP algorithm for decoding, similar to those in
[
7
,
6
]
. Starting with the initial estimate
\boldsymbol{x}^{0}=\boldsymbol{0}
of the joint message vector
\boldsymbol{x}
, for
t\geq 0
, the AMP decoder recursively computes:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{z}^{t}=\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{x}^{t}+\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{t}\odot\boldsymbol{z}^{t-1},

(35)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}^{t+1}=\eta_{t}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{s}^{t}=\boldsymbol{x}^{t}+\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{t}\odot\boldsymbol{A}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{z}^{t}.

(36)

Here
\eta_{t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{LM}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{LM}
is a Lipschitz denoising function that produces an updated estimate
\boldsymbol{x}^{t+1}
of
\boldsymbol{x}
from the effective observation
\boldsymbol{s}^{t}
.
The operator
\odot
is the Hadamard (entrywise) product, and quantities with negative iteration index are set to all-zero vectors which means
\boldsymbol{z}^{0}=\boldsymbol{y}-\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{x}^{0}=\boldsymbol{y}
.
Before giving the exact form of the vector
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, the matrix
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times LM}
and the denoiser
\eta_{t}
in (
36
), we explain the asymptotic distributional properties of the AMP algorithm via a deterministic recursion called
state evolution
.

State evolution (SE)

Recall that the joint message vector
\boldsymbol{x}
consists of
L
length-
M
sections
\boldsymbol{x}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{x}_{L}
drawn i.i.d. from
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}
. Each section corresponds to a specific column block
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, so there are
L/\textsf{C}
sections per column block. Let
\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{L}
denote the
L
sections of
\boldsymbol{s}^{t}
.
For each section
\ell
located in block
c
, the debiasing term
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{t}\odot\boldsymbol{z}^{t-1}
in (
36
) ensures that for
n,L\to\infty
with
\mu=L/n\in(0,\infty)
, and
t\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\right)\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(\boldsymbol{0},\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\boldsymbol{I})\ ,

(37)

where the noise variance
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}

can be characterized through the following SE recursion. Initialized with
\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{0}=E_{b}k
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, the SE iteratively computes for
t\geq 0
,
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
:

\displaystyle\phi_{\textsf{r}}^{t}=\sigma^{2}+\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\ ,

(38)

\displaystyle\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}=\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}\frac{W_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}}{\phi_{\textsf{r}}^{t}}\right]^{-1}\ ,

(39)

\displaystyle\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1}=\mathbb{E}\big\|\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}-\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)\big\|_{2}^{2}\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(\boldsymbol{0},\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\boldsymbol{I})\ ,

(40)

where
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
denotes the denoising function associated with block
c
(see (
3.2
)).
Using the parameters in the SE equations, we can define
the vector
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and the matrix
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times LM}
in (
35
)–(
36
) as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{i}^{t}=\frac{\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}}\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}{\phi_{\textsf{r}(i)^{t-1}}},\quad\tilde{Q}_{ij}^{t}=\frac{\tau_{\textsf{c}(j)}^{t}}{\phi_{\textsf{r}(i)}^{t}}\quad\text{for}\;i\in[n],\;j\in[LM]\ .

(41)

The parameter
\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1}
can be interpreted as the MSE for a section located in block
c
.
Since each block in the spatially coupled matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
has different variance, the Hadamard product with
\boldsymbol{Q}
in (
36
) takes this into account and adjusts the matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
accordingly.

Choice of denoiser
\eta_{t}
in AMP

The distributional characterization in (
37
) motivates applying the denoiser
\eta_{t}
in (
36
) section-wise to its input, i.e., for
t\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\eta_{t}(\boldsymbol{s}^{t})=\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t,1}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{1}\right)\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{t,1}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{L/\textsf{C}}\right)\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{t,\textsf{C}}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{(\textsf{C}-1)L/\textsf{C}+1}\right)\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{t,\textsf{C}}\left(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{L}\right)\end{bmatrix}\begin{array}[]{ c }\left.\kern-1.2pt\vphantom{\begin{array}[]{ c }\eta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{1}\\
\end{array}}\right\}\text{$\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}$ sections with $\textsf{c}=1$}\\
\\
\vphantom{\vdots}\left.\kern-1.2pt\vphantom{\begin{array}[]{ c }\eta_{\textsf{C}}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{\textsf{C}}\\
\end{array}}\right\}\text{$\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}$ sections with $\textsf{c}=\textsf{C}$.}\end{array}

Here, for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
,
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
denotes the denoiser for block
c
, and is applied to each of the
\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}
sections located in block
c
. We choose
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}
to be either the Bayes-optimal estimator or the marginal-MMSE estimator for each section of the joint message vector
\boldsymbol{x}
in block
c
.
These two estimators are described next.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}\coloneqq\left\{(\textsf{c}-1){L}/{\textsf{C}}+1,\dots,\textsf{c}{L}/{\textsf{C}}\right\}
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
. Then (
37
) says that section
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}
of the effective observation vector in iteration
t\geq 1
,
\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t}
, is asymptotically distributed as the signal
\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}
embedded in i.i.d. Gaussian noise with variance
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
.
Hence, the Bayes-optimal denoiser for block
c
, denoted by
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
and applied to
\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t}
, is the MMSE estimator taking the form:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\textrm{Bayes}}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}^{t}_{\ell})=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}\big|\ \boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}=\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{M}\sqrt{E}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\cdot\frac{\frac{\alpha}{M}\exp\left({(s_{\ell}^{t})_{j}\sqrt{E}}/{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}-E/(2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\right)}{\frac{\alpha}{M}\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{M}\exp\left((s_{\ell}^{t})_{j^{\prime}}\sqrt{E}/\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}-E/(2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\right)+(1-\alpha)}\,,

(49)

where we recall
E=E_{b}k
and
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
is the
j
-th canonical basis vector.

A suboptimal alternative to the Bayes-optimal denoiser is the marginal-MMSE denoiser, denoted by
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
. This denoiser acts entrywise on its input and computes the entrywise conditional expectation using the marginal distribution of
\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}
and
\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}^{t+1}=\eta^{\textrm{marginal}}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{x}_{\sec,1}|\ \bar{x}_{\sec,1}+g_{\textsf{c},1}^{t}=(s_{\ell}^{t})_{1}\right]\\
\vdots\\
\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{x}_{\sec,M}|\ \bar{x}_{\sec,M}+g_{\textsf{c},M}^{t}=(s_{\ell}^{t})_{M}\right]\end{bmatrix},

(50)

with

\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{x}_{\sec,j}|\ \bar{x}_{\sec,j}+g_{\textsf{c},j}^{t}=(s_{\ell}^{t})_{j}\right]=\sqrt{E}\cdot\frac{\frac{\alpha}{M}\exp\left((s_{\ell}^{t})_{j}\sqrt{E}/\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}-E/(2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\right)}{\frac{\alpha}{M}\exp\left((s^{t}_{\ell})_{j}\sqrt{E}/\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}-E/(2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\right)+\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{M}\right)}\,,\quad j\in[M].

In the next subsection (Section
3.3
), we will characterize the asymptotic performance of the AMP decoder with
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
and with
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}
. While
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}
leads to worse performance than
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
, its asymptotic performance can be computed much more efficiently than of
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
.

Hard-decision step in AMP

In each iteration
t\geq 1
, given the effective observation
\boldsymbol{s}^{t}
, the AMP decoder can produce a hard-decision estimate
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}^{t+1}
of the joint message vector
\boldsymbol{x}
. Let
h_{t,\textsf{c}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
be the hard-decision function for block
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, which applies section-wise to the
\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}
sections in block
c
. To minimize the probability of detection error, we choose
h_{t,\textsf{c}}
to be the MAP estimator, i.e., for section
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}
, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{t+1}=h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})=\argmax_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}=\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\mid\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}=\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t}\right),

(51)

where
\mathcal{X}=\{\sqrt{E}\,\boldsymbol{e}_{j},\,\forall j\in[B]\}\cup\boldsymbol{0}
is the support of
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}
. We can then calculate the error probabilities
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
by comparing the final estimate
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{t}
against the ground truth
\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}
, for
\ell\in[L]
. Note that the entrywise variance
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
of
\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
depends on the choice of the sequence of denoising functions
\eta_{0,\textsf{c}},\dots,\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}
, as stated in (
38
)–(
40
). This implies that
h_{t,\textsf{c}}
uses a different value for
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
, depending on whether we use the Bayes-optimal denoiser or the marginal-MMSE denoiser.

3.3
Asymptotic Error Analysis for AMP Decoding

We characterize the asymptotic error probabilities
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
of the random coding scheme in Section
3.1

with spatially coupled design matrix under AMP decoding, in the limit as
n,L\to\infty
with
L/n\to\mu\in(0,\infty)
. We consider two AMP decoders with
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
as denoiser.
To quantify their error probabilities, we need to characterize the fixed point of the AMP decoders in the limit
t\to\infty
. We do this using the stationary points of suitable potential functions, following the approach in
[
32
,
52
]
. We will bound the
error probabilities for each decoder in terms of the minimizer of a potential function. The potential function corresponding to
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
is similar to the one used in
[
7
]
, and the potential function for
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
is similar to that in
[
6
]
.

Potential function for AMP with
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}

Consider the following single-section channel, with output
\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{M}
given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau}=\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}+\sqrt{\tau}\boldsymbol{z}\ \in{\mathbb{R}}^{M},

(52)

where
\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}
and
\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I})
independent of
\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}
.
Then the potential function for the AMP decoder with
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
is defined as
[
7
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2},\psi\right)=I(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec};\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau})+\frac{1}{2\mu}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\sigma^{2}}\right)-\frac{\mu\psi}{\tau}\right],

(53)

where
\mu=L/n
is the user density,
\sigma^{2}
is the channel noise of the GMAC,
\psi\in[0,E]
and
\tau=\sigma^{2}+\mu\psi
. The mutual information
I(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec};\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau})
is in base-
e
and can be computed using (
52
) to be:

\displaystyle I(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec};\boldsymbol{s}_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\alpha\ln\left(\frac{M}{\alpha}\right)-(1-\alpha)\ln(1-\alpha)

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha\left(\ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+\frac{E}{2\tau}\right)-\alpha\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\alpha}{M}\sum_{j=2}^{M}e^{\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}z_{j}-\frac{E}{2\tau}}+\frac{\alpha}{M}e^{\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}z_{1}+\frac{E}{2\tau}}+1-\alpha\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\alpha)\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{z}}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\alpha}{M(1-\alpha)}\sum_{j=1}^{M}e^{\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}z_{j}-\frac{E}{2\tau}}+1\right)\right].

(54)

Define the largest minimizer of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
w.r.t.
\psi
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\textrm{Bayes}}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2}\right)=\max\Bigg\{\argmin_{\psi\in[0,E]}\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2},\psi\right)\Bigg\}.

(55)

This will be used later in this subsection to characterize the asymptotic error probabilities of the AMP decoder that uses
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
.

Potential function for AMP with
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}

Consider the following scalar channel, with output
s_{\tau}\in{\mathbb{R}}
given by

\displaystyle s_{\tau}=\bar{x}_{\sec}+\sqrt{\tau}z\ \in{\mathbb{R}},

(56)

where
\bar{x}_{\sec}
is distributed as the entrywise marginal of
\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}
, i.e.,

\bar{x}_{\sec}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{E}&\text{with probability }\frac{\alpha}{M}\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

The noise
z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
is independent of
\bar{x}_{\sec}
. Then the potential function
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
for the AMP decoder with
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
is defined as
[
6
]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2},\psi\right)=I(\bar{x}_{\sec};s_{\tau})+\frac{1}{2\mu M}\left[\ln\left(\frac{\tau}{\sigma^{2}}\right)-\frac{\mu\psi}{\tau}\right],

(57)

where
\mu,\sigma^{2}
,
\psi
and
\tau
are as defined in (
53
), and
I(\bar{x}_{\sec};s_{\tau})
can be computed as

\displaystyle I(\bar{x}_{\sec};s_{\tau})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{M}\ln\left(\frac{M}{\alpha}\right)-\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{M}\right)\ln\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{M}\right)-\frac{\alpha}{M}\mathbb{E}_{z}\left[\ln\left(1+\left(\frac{M}{\alpha}-1\right)e^{\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}z-\frac{E}{2\tau}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\quad-\left(1-\frac{\alpha}{M}\right)\left\{\mathbb{E}_{z}\left[\ln\left(e^{\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}z-\frac{E}{2\tau}}+\frac{M}{\alpha}-1\right)\right]-\ln\left(\frac{M}{\alpha}-1\right)\right\}.

(58)

Similarly to (
55
), we define the largest minimizer of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
w.r.t.
\psi
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\textrm{marginal}}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2}\right)=\max\Bigg\{\argmin_{\psi\in[0,E]}\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2},\psi\right)\Bigg\}.

(59)

Using the definitions above, we can state the following result, which will be used to characterize the fixed point of each AMP decoder.

Lemma 1

(Fixed points of state evolution)
.

Consider the state evolution recursion in (
38
)–(
40
), defined via an
(\omega,\Lambda)
base matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
(Definition
1
), and
\eta_{t}
given by either
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
. Then, for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, the SE parameter
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
is non-increasing in
t
and converges to a fixed point
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{\infty}
, which satisfies the following.

For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists
\omega_{0}<\infty
and
\Lambda_{0}<\infty
such that for all
\omega>\omega_{0}
and
\Lambda>\Lambda_{0}
, the fixed points
\{\tau^{\infty}_{\textsf{c}}\}_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}
satisfy

\displaystyle\max_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{\infty}\leq{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}:=\sigma^{2}+\mu_{\text{in}}\left(\mathcal{M}\left(\mu_{\text{in}},\sigma^{2}\right)+\epsilon\right),

(60)

where
\mu_{\text{in}}=\vartheta\mu
,
\vartheta:=\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}=1+\frac{\omega-1}{\Lambda}
, and
\mathcal{M}(\mu_{\text{in}},\sigma^{2})=\mathcal{M}_{\textrm{Bayes}}(\mu_{\text{in}},\sigma^{2})
when
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}=\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
, or
\mathcal{M}(\mu_{\text{in}},\sigma^{2})=\mathcal{M}_{\textrm{marginal}}(\mu_{\text{in}},\sigma^{2})
when
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}=\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega\to\infty}\lim_{\Lambda\to\infty}{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}\to\sigma^{2}+\mu\left(\mathcal{M}\left(\mu,\sigma^{2}\right)+\epsilon\right).

(61)

Figure 6:
Potential functions
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
vs. normalized MSE
\psi/E
. Different colours correspond to different values of
E_{b}/N_{0}
. Here
k=6,\alpha=0.7
,
\mu=0.28
.

We discuss the implications of Lemma
1
before giving the proof.
Consider the AMP decoder in (
35
)–(
36
) for solving the linear estimation problem in (
31
) with a spatially coupled Gaussian design. Recall from (
37
) that the asymptotic noise variance in the effective observation for each iteration
t\geq 0
is quantified by the SE parameters
\{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\}_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}
, where
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
indexes the column blocks. Also recall that these SE parameters depend on the denoiser used by the AMP decoder, i.e.,
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
. Given a user density
\mu
, Lemma
1
says that at the fixed point of the AMP decoder, the effective noise variance in all column blocks is asymptotically upper bounded using the largest minimizer of the corresponding potential function
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
for user density
\mu_{\text{in}}=\ \vartheta\mu
, where we note that
\mu_{\text{in}}\to\mu
in the limit of large base matrices (i.e.,
\Lambda\to\infty
then
\omega\to\infty
).

Using (
40
), the upper bound on the asymptotic effective noise variance
\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{\infty}
in Lemma
1
can be converted into an upper bound on
\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{\infty}
, the asymptotic MSE for block
c
achieved at the fixed point of the AMP decoder.
We illustrate this result in Fig.
6
, where we plot the potential functions
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}\equiv\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}(\mu,\sigma^{2},\psi)
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}\equiv\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}(\mu,\sigma^{2},\psi)
against
\psi/E\in[0,1]
, for
\mu=0.28
and a few different values of
E_{b}/N_{0}
(varied through
\sigma^{2}
). Different colours represent different values of
E_{b}/N_{0}
. The dashed curves correspond to
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
and the solid curves correspond to
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
. The global minimizers of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
are marked by circles and crosses, respectively. These minimizers upper bound
\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{\infty}/E
, the asymptotic normalized MSE achievable by the AMP decoder.

Observe that for a lower SNR, e.g.,
E_{b}/N_{0}=2
dB,
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
each has a unique minimizer, and these minimizers coincide at a high normalized MSE (NMSE) of around
0.5.

When the SNR increases to
4.73
dB, while
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
still has a unique minimizer at
\text{NMSE}\approx 0.5
,
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
exhibits two minimizers, one at
\text{NMSE}\approx 0.5
and the other at
\text{NMSE}\approx 0.02
. Nevertheless, the largest minimizers of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
still coincide at
0.5
, indicating that the NMSE achievable by the AMP decoder, using either
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
or
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
as its denoiser, is upper bounded by
0.5
.

As the SNR further increases to
E_{b}/N_{0}=5.15
dB or
6.73
dB, the minimizers of the potential functions shift towards smaller NMSE values, reaching as low as
10^{-4}
. This implies that the AMP decoder achieves smaller NMSE for higher SNRs.
Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
begin to exhibit distinct minimizers, with the minimizer of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
consistently being smaller than that of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
. This indicates that using
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
instead of
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
as the denoiser allows the AMP decoder to converge to a smaller NMSE, as one would expect.

Proof of Lemma
1
.

This result is similar to
[
7
, Theorem 2]
and
[
6
, Lemma IV.1]
, and can be proved using the same techniques.
Omitting the details, we sketch the key steps of the proof. Defining
\gamma_{\textsf{r}}^{t}:=\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\psi_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
for
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
, the SE recursion (
38
)–(
40
) associated with the spatially-coupled system can be rewritten into a single-line recursion:

\displaystyle\gamma_{\textsf{r}}^{t+1}=\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\mathrm{mmse}\left(\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}=1}^{\textsf{R}}\,\frac{W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}}{\sigma^{2}+\mu_{\text{in}}\gamma_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}}\right)\quad\text{for}\;\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}],

(62)

where the function
\mathrm{mmse}(\cdot)
is defined differently depending on the denoiser used in the AMP decoder.
For the Bayes-optimal denoiser, we have
\mathrm{mmse}(1/\tau):=\mathbb{E}\|\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}-\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}\mid\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}+\sqrt{\tau}\boldsymbol{z}\right]\|_{2}^{2}
with
\boldsymbol{z}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{I})
, and for the marginal-MMSE denoiser, we have
\mathrm{mmse}(1/\tau):=\mathbb{E}(\bar{x}_{\sec}-\mathbb{E}\left[\bar{x}_{\sec}\mid\bar{x}_{\sec}+\sqrt{\tau}z\right])^{2}
with
z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
.

The SE recursion for the corresponding
uncoupled
system can be obtained by substituting
W=1
in (
38
)–(
40
) and dropping the block indices, resulting in the following single-line recursion:

\displaystyle\gamma^{t+1}=\psi^{t+1}=\mathrm{mmse}\left(\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}+\mu_{\text{in}}\psi^{t}}\right),

(63)

where the function
\mathrm{mmse}(\cdot)
for each denoiser is defined as in (
62
). The recursions (
62
) and (
63
) correspond exactly to those in
[
52
, Eqs. (27)–(28)]
. Hence, by
[
52
, Theorem 1]
, the fixed points of the coupled recursion in (
62
) exist and can be bounded from above using the largest minimizer of the potential function corresponding to the uncoupled recursion in (
63
). This potential function can be computed using the formula in
[
52
, Eq. (4)]
, and
takes the form of
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
in (
53
) or
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
in (
57
), depending on whether
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
is the denoiser. Finally the bounds on the fixed points
\{\gamma^{\infty}_{\textsf{r}}\}_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}
can be translated to bounds on
\{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{\infty}\}_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}
using the same arguments as in the proof of
[
7
, Theorem 2]
.
∎

Using the characterization of the asymptotic effective noise variance in Lemma
1
, we can bound the asymptotic error probabilities
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
achieved by the AMP decoder.

Theorem 2

(Asymptotic achievability bounds)
.

Consider the coding scheme described in Section
3.1
,
with a spatially coupled design
\boldsymbol{A}
constructed using an
(\omega,\Lambda)
base matrix, and the AMP decoder in (
35
)–(
36
) with
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}=\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}=\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}
.
Let the base matrix parameters
\omega>\omega_{0}
and
\Lambda>\Lambda_{0}
, where

\omega_{0},\Lambda_{0}
are given by Lemma
1
. Let
{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}
be as defined in (
60
), which takes different forms depending on whether the AMP decoder uses
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
or
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
as the denoiser.
Recall that
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}^{t+1}
be the hard-decision estimate of
\boldsymbol{x}
that the AMP decoder returns in iteration
t
as defined in (
51
).
Define also
\xi(\tau):=\ln\left(\frac{M}{\alpha}(1-\alpha)\right)/\sqrt{E/\tau}
, and

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\Phi\left(\xi(\tau)-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}\right)\Phi\left(\xi(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}\right)^{M-1},

(64)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=\left[\frac{\alpha\left(1-\Phi\left(\xi(\tau)-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}\right)\Phi\left(\xi(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}\right)^{M-1}\right)}{(1-\alpha)\left(1-\Phi\left(\xi(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}\right)^{M}\right)}+1\right]^{-1},

(65)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=1-\mathbb{E}_{z}\left[\Phi\left(\max\left\{\xi(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}},z+\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau}}\right\}\right)^{M-1}\right],\quad z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1),

(66)

where
\Phi(\cdot)
denotes the cumulative density function of the standard Gaussian.
Fix any
\delta>0
, and let
T
denote the first iteration for which
\max_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{T}\leq{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}+\delta
.
Then the
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
achieved by the AMP decoder in iteration
T
satisfy the following almost surely:

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}p_{\mathrm{MD}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]:\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbbm{1}\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{T+1}=\boldsymbol{0}\}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}\left(h_{T,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{T})=\boldsymbol{0}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}({\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}+\delta),

(67)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}p_{\mathrm{FA}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]:\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{T+1}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbbm{1}\{\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}\}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{=}}\left[\frac{\alpha\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}(h_{T,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{T})\neq\boldsymbol{0})}{(1-\alpha)\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}(h_{T,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{g}^{T}_{\textsf{c}})\neq\boldsymbol{0})}+1\right]^{-1}\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}({\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}+\delta),

(68)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}p_{\mathrm{AUE}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]:\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbbm{1}\big\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{T+1}\,\notin\{\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{0}\}\big\}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{a.s.}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}\left(h_{T,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{T}\right)\notin\{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}},\boldsymbol{0}\}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}({\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}+\delta),

(69)

where the limits are taken with
L/n\to\mu
held constant.

Proof.

The first equality in each of (
67
)–(
69
) follows from the definition of
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in (
3
)–(
5
). The second equality in each of in (
3
)–(
5
) can be shown using the state evolution convergence result and a sandwiching argument, analogous to the proof of
[
7
, Theorem 1]
. As an example, we sketch the proof of the second equality in (
67
).
Applying results from
[
21
,
53
,
34
]
, the joint empirical distribution of the AMP iterates converges as follows, to a law specified by the state evolution parameters. For any Lipschitz test function
\varphi:{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
t\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\varphi(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\varphi(\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sec},\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sec}+\boldsymbol{g}^{t}_{\textsf{c}})\right],\quad\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{M}(\boldsymbol{0},\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\boldsymbol{I}),

(70)

where we recall that
\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}=\{(\textsf{c}-1)L/\textsf{C}+1,\dots,\textsf{c}L/\textsf{C}\}
.
The claim in (
67
) requires a test function
\varphi
that is defined via indicator functions, which are not Lipschitz. We handle this by sandwich
the required function
\varphi
between two Lipschitz functions
\varphi_{\epsilon,-}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})
,
\varphi_{\epsilon,+}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})
that both converge to
\varphi
as
\epsilon\to 0
). This allows us to apply (
70
) to
\varphi_{\epsilon,-}
and
\varphi_{\epsilon,+}
, and translate them to
\varphi
by taking
\epsilon\to 0
) and applying the dominated convergence theorem. We refer the reader to the proofs of
[
7
, Theorem 1]
or
[
35
, Theorem 1]
for details of the sandwich argument. Hence, for
t\geq 1
we almost surely have

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}\cdot\frac{1}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]:\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbbm{1}\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{t+1}=\boldsymbol{0}\}

\displaystyle=\left[\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}\frac{L}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\right]\cdot\left[\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbbm{1}\{\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\;\text{and}\;\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{t+1}=\boldsymbol{0}\}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(a)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\alpha}\cdot\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\;\text{and}\;h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})=\boldsymbol{0}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(b)}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})=\boldsymbol{0}\right),

(71)

where (a) holds by the strong law of large numbers, and (b) by (
70
) combined with the sandwich argument.

In the rest of the proof, we prove the last inequality in each of (
67
)–(
69
). Recall from (
51
)–(
52
) that for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}
, the hard-decision step in iteration
t\geq 1
takes the form:

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})=\argmax_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}=\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\,|\,\ \boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}+\boldsymbol{g}^{t}_{\textsf{c}}=\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t}\right)

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\{\ln p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec}}(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})+\frac{(\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}-\frac{(\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime})^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\argmax_{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\{\ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+\frac{(\boldsymbol{s}_{\ell}^{t})^{\top}\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}-\frac{E}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}\;\text{ for}\;\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\ ,\;\ \ln(1-\alpha)\;\text{ for}\;\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{0}\right\},

(72)

where we recall
\mathcal{X}=\{\sqrt{E}\boldsymbol{e}_{j},\,\forall j\in[M]\}\cup\boldsymbol{0}
. Using (
72
) and assuming without loss of generality that
\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}=\sqrt{E}\boldsymbol{e}_{1}
, we first derive the expressions for
\mathbb{P}(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})=\boldsymbol{0})
,
\mathbb{P}(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\neq\boldsymbol{0})
and
\mathbb{P}(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\neq\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}})
, which form the left side of the inequalities in (
67
)–(
69
).

Letting
z_{1},\ldots,z_{M}\sim_{\text{i.i.d.}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
and
\xi(\tau)=\ln\left(\frac{M}{\alpha}(1-\alpha)\right)/\sqrt{E/\tau}
, the hard-decision rule in (
72
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})=\boldsymbol{0}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\ln(1-\alpha)>\max\left\{\ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+z_{1}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}+\frac{E}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}\ ,\ \ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+z_{j}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}-\frac{E}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}\;\,\text{for all }2\leq j\leq M\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(z_{1}<\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right)\prod_{j=2}^{M}\mathbb{P}\left(z_{j}<\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi\left(\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right)\Phi\left(\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right)^{M-1}.

(73)

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)\notin\{\boldsymbol{\bar{x}}_{\sec,\mathrm{a}},\boldsymbol{0}\}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+z_{j}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}-\frac{E}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}>\max\left\{\ln(1-\alpha),\ \ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+z_{1}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}+\frac{E}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}\right\},\;\text{for some }2\leq j\leq M\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left(z_{j}<\max\left\{\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\ ,\ z_{1}+\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right\},\;\text{for all }j\geq 2\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{E}_{z_{1}}\left[\Phi\left(\max\left\{\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\ ,\ z_{1}+\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right\}\right)^{M-1}\right],

(74)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)\neq\boldsymbol{0}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{g}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)=\boldsymbol{0}\right)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{P}\left(\ln(1-\alpha)>\ln\frac{\alpha}{M}+z_{j}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}-\frac{E}{2\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}\,,\;\text{for all }j\in[M]\right)

\displaystyle=1-\Phi\left(\xi(\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t})+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{E}{\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right)^{M}.

(75)

Finally, by substituting (
73
)–(
75
) into the left side of each inequality in (
67
)–(
69
), and applying the result from Lemma
1
along with
\max_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{T}\leq{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}+\delta
for some
\delta>0
, we obtain the upper bounds on the asymptotic error probabilities.
∎

When all the users are always active, i.e.,
\alpha=1
, the schemes based on random coding and AMP decoding, along with their asymptotic error analysis, reduce to their counterparts in
[
7
,
6
]
. The error probabilities
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
vanish, leaving
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
as the only nonzero error probability, which is equivalent to the per-user probability of error,
\mathtt{PUPE}:=\mathbb{E}\big[\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbbm{1}\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}^{T+1}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\}\big]
. Formally, Corollary
2
below follows from Theorem
2
by taking
\alpha=1
.

Corollary 2

(Asymptotic achievability bound for
\alpha=1
)
.

Consider the setting of Theorem
2
with
\alpha=1
. Let
{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}
be as defined in (
60
),
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}^{t+1}
be the hard-decision estimate of
\boldsymbol{x}
in iteration
t
, defined in (
51
).
Fix any
\delta>0
, and let
T
denote the first iteration for which
\max_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{T}\leq{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}+\delta
.
Then in iteration
T
, the per-user probability of error (
\mathtt{PUPE}
) achieved by the AMP decoder satisfies the following almost surely:

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathtt{PUPE}

\displaystyle=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\mathbbm{1}\{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{T+1}\neq\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\}\right]\leq 1-\mathbb{E}_{z}\left[\Phi\left(z+\sqrt{\frac{E}{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}+\delta}}\right)^{M-1}\right],

(76)

where
z\sim{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)
, and the limit is taken with
L/n\to\mu
held constant.

Recall that
{\bar{\tau}_{\vartheta}}
takes different values depending on the denoiser used by the AMP decoder.
With
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
as the denoiser, (
76
) matches the bound by Hsieh et al.
[
7
]
; see equations (22) and (37) in
[
7
]
. When the AMP decoder employs
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
as its denoiser,
Eq. (
76
)
offers a tighter upper bound on
\mathtt{PUPE}
than the bound by Kowshik in
[
6
, Thm. IV.3]
. This is because while our bound assumes the optimal section-wise MAP rule (
51
) in the hard-decision step, the latter uses the suboptimal entrywise MAP rule, coupled with the following union bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\ell}^{t+1}\neq\boldsymbol{x}_{\ell}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left((\hat{x}_{\ell}^{t+1})_{j}\neq(x_{\ell})_{j}\ \text{for any }j\in[M]\right)\leq\sum_{j=1}^{M}\mathbb{P}\left((\hat{x}_{\ell}^{t+1})_{j}\neq(x_{\ell})_{j}\right).

(77)

Moreover, both the bound in (
76
) and that in
[
6
, Thm. IV.3]
involve only the distribution function of the standard normal or its expectation, and are thus equally efficient to compute for larger
k
.

3.4
Numerical Results

We numerically evaluate the asymptotic error bounds on
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in Theorem
2
as the system size
n,L\to\infty
with
L/n\to\mu
held constant. We also consider the limit of large base matrices, with
\Lambda\to\infty
and then
\omega\to\infty
(hence
\vartheta=1+\frac{\omega-1}{\Lambda}\to 1
and
\mu_{\text{in}}=\vartheta\mu\to\mu
).
Our Python code is available at
[
54
]
, and implementation details are given in Appendix
A
.

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}}
,

then

,

\delta\to 0
in (

)–(

).

. The achievability bound of Hsieh et al.

). Maximum

k

. Observe that the error bounds decrease with increasing

between

for

dB in Fig.

, the magnitude of the gap is very small (e.g.,
10^{-4}
).
As a result, the error bounds associated with

or

nearly coincide with each other for

dB in Fig.

.

Moreover, we observe that
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}

and

in Fig.

, which is consistent with the behaviour of the finite-length bounds in Theorem
1
(see Fig.

). Indeed, both the finite-length and the asymptotic bounds are based on random coding, the former using an i.i.d. design matrix and the latter a spatially coupled design. This partially explains why the two bounds exhibit the same trend.

in Section

\mu_{\mathrm{a}}=\alpha\mu

for a target total error
\max\{p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+p_{\mathrm{AUE}}\leq 0.01
, obtained by evaluating the bounds in Theorem

. The results are shown for
\alpha=0.7
, and
k=6
or
k=60
.

\alpha=1
), Fig.

) for
\mathtt{PUPE}
using

[

and Kowshik

.
