(solid red).

4
Efficient CDMA-
T
ype Coding Scheme

The two achievability bounds in Theorems
1
and
2
above are based on random coding schemes which cannot be implemented for user payloads larger than a few bits, e.g.,
k>8
. This is because the size
M=2^{k}
of the users’ codebooks grows exponentially in
k
, posing challenges in both memory and decoding complexity.
In this section, we propose an efficient CDMA-type coding scheme with decoding complexity that is linear in the payload
k
, and provide performance guarantees that can be compared with the achievability bounds in Theorems
1
and
2
.

4.1
CDMA-
T
ype Coding Scheme

As in Section
3
, we consider the simple prior where each user
\ell\in[L]
is independently active with probability
\alpha
. Let
\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
denote the message of user
\ell
. We assume

\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}
for
\ell\in[L]
, where
\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
has the distribution

\displaystyle p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}=(1-\alpha)\delta_{\boldsymbol{0}}+\alpha\,p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}}\,.

(78)

Here,
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}}
is a uniform distribution on the set of BPSK sequences
\mathcal{X}_{\mathrm{a}}=\{\pm\sqrt{E_{b}}\}^{k}
, where we recall that
E_{b}
is the energy per bit.

Recall that
k
is fixed, and does not grow with
n
and
L
. To construct the codeword of user
\ell
, we first compute the outer product of a signature sequence
\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\frac{n}{k}}
with
\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}
to obtain the matrix
\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\frac{n}{k}\times k}
. The codeword
\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}
of user
\ell
is then obtained as
\boldsymbol{c}_{\ell}=\mathrm{vectorize}(\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

For simplicity, let
\tilde{n}=\frac{n}{k}
, and define the matrices
\boldsymbol{A}=[\boldsymbol{a}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{a}_{L}]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times L}
and
\boldsymbol{X}=[\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{X}_{L}]^{\top}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k}
.
Then the GMAC channel output in (
1
) can be rewritten in matrix form as

\boldsymbol{Y}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\,\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k},

(79)

where
{\mathcal{E}}_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\sigma^{2})
for
i\in\tilde{n}
and
j\in[k]
is the channel noise.
See Fig.
9
for an illustration.

This coding scheme can be straightforwardly adapted to more complicated priors
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}
, including those that model higher-order modulations such as QPSK instead of BPSK.

Spatially coupled design

We use a spatially coupled design for
\boldsymbol{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times L}
, the matrix of signature sequences. It is defined similarly to the one in Section
3.1
, with independent zero-mean Gaussian entries whose variances are specified by a base matrix
\boldsymbol{W}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\textsf{R}\times\textsf{C}}
.
More precisely,

A_{i\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}\bigg(0,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}/\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}(\ell)}\bigg),\quad\text{for }i\in[\tilde{n}],\ \ell\in[L].

(80)

Similarly to Section
3.1
, the operators
\textsf{r}(\cdot):[\tilde{n}]\rightarrow[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}(\cdot):[L]\rightarrow[\textsf{C}]
here map a particular row or column index of
\boldsymbol{A}
to its corresponding row block or column block index. This construction ensures the columns of
\boldsymbol{A}
have unit squared
\ell_{2}
-norm asymptotically, and therefore the energy-per-bit constraint is satisfied asymptotically since
\|\boldsymbol{C}_{\ell}\|_{F}^{2}/k=\|\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell}\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{\top}\|_{F}^{2}/k=\|\boldsymbol{a}_{\ell}\|_{2}^{2}E_{b}\to E_{b}
as
\tilde{n}\to\infty
.
Our theoretical analysis applies to a generic base matrix
\boldsymbol{W}
. For the numerical results, we use the
(\omega,\Lambda)
base matrix in Definition
1
.

Figure 9:
CDMA-type coding scheme.

4.2
AMP Decoding and State Evolution

This section describes an AMP decoder to recover
\boldsymbol{X}
from
\boldsymbol{Y}
given the spatially coupled matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
, and provides the asymptotic guarantees on its error performance. A key difference from the AMP decoder in Section
3.2
is that the signal
\boldsymbol{X}
in (
79
) is a matrix with a row-wise i.i.d. prior, rather than a vector with a section-wise prior.

Starting with an initializer
\boldsymbol{X}^{0}=\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right],\,...\,,\,\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right]\right]^{\top}=\boldsymbol{0}_{L\times k}
, the decoder computes the following in iteration
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}^{t}+\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}^{t},

(81)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{X}^{t+1}=\eta_{t}\left(\boldsymbol{S}^{t}\right),\quad\text{where}\quad\boldsymbol{S}^{t}=\boldsymbol{X}^{t}+\boldsymbol{V}^{t}.

(82)

Here
\boldsymbol{X}^{t+1}
is an updated estimate of
\boldsymbol{X}
produced using a denoising function
\eta_{t}:\mathbb{R}^{L\times k}\to\mathbb{R}^{L\times k}
, and
\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}
can be viewed as a modified residual. The denoiser is assumed to be Lipschitz and separable, acting row-wise on matrix inputs:

\displaystyle\eta_{t}(\boldsymbol{S})=\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{t,1}\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{1}\right)^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{t,1}\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{L/\textsf{C}}\right)^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{t,\textsf{C}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{(\textsf{C}-1)L/\textsf{C}+1}\right)^{\top}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{t,\textsf{C}}\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{L}\right)^{\top}\end{bmatrix}\begin{array}[]{ c }\left.\kern-1.2pt\vphantom{\begin{array}[]{ c }\eta_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{1}\\
\end{array}}\right\}\text{$\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}$ users with $\textsf{c}=1$}\\
\\
\vphantom{\vdots}\left.\kern-1.2pt\vphantom{\begin{array}[]{ c }\eta_{\textsf{C}}\\
\vdots\\
\eta_{\textsf{C}}\\
\end{array}}\right\}\text{$\frac{L}{\textsf{C}}$ users with $\textsf{c}=\textsf{C}$.}\end{array}

Here
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
corresponds to the denoiser for users in block
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
.

For
t\geq 0
,
\tilde{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{t}
and
\boldsymbol{V}^{t}
in (
81
)–(
82
) are defined through a matrix

\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}\times k\textsf{C}}
, which consists of
\textsf{R}\times\textsf{C}
submatrices, each of size
k\times k
. For
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}],\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, the submatrix
\boldsymbol{Q}_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
is defined as:

\boldsymbol{Q}_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}^{t}=\left[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\right]^{-1}\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\,,

(90)

where
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
are deterministic matrices defined later in (
97
)–(
99
).
The
i
th row of matrix
\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k}
then takes the form:

\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}_{i}^{t}={k\mu_{\text{in}}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}}\cdot\frac{1}{L/{\textsf{C}}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}^{\prime}\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{\ell}^{t-1}\right)\,\big[\boldsymbol{Q}^{t-1}_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}}\big]^{\top}\,\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t-1}\,,\quad\text{for }i\in[\tilde{n}],

(91)

where
\mu_{\text{in}}=\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\mu
and
\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}=\{(\textsf{c}-1)L/\textsf{C}+1,\dots,\textsf{c}L/\textsf{C}\}
. Here

\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{s})=\frac{\textrm{d}\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})}{\textrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
is the derivative (Jacobian) of
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}
, and quantities with negative iteration index are set to all-zero matrices which means at iteration
t=0
,

\boldsymbol{Z}^{0}=\boldsymbol{Y}-\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}^{0}.

(92)

The
\ell
th row of
\boldsymbol{V}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k}
takes the form:

\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}A_{i,\ell}\Big[\boldsymbol{Q}_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}(\ell)}^{t}\Big]^{\top}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t}\,,\quad\text{for }\ell\in[L].

(93)

Remark 1
.

We have chosen our notation to highlight the similarities between the AMP decoder in (
81
)–(
82
) and the one in (
35
)–(
36
) in Section
3.2
. i) The effective observation
\boldsymbol{S}^{t}
in (
82
) defined via (
93
) is similar to the expression for
\boldsymbol{s}^{t}
in (
36
). ii) The debiasing term
\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}^{t}
in (
81
) defined in (
91
) is also analogous to the debiasing term
\tilde{\boldsymbol{v}}^{t}\odot\boldsymbol{z}^{t-1}
in (
35
), by noticing that
one may rewrite the expression for
\tilde{v}^{t}_{i}
in (
41
) as

\displaystyle\tilde{v}_{i}^{t}=M\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}}\cdot\frac{1}{LM/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\sum_{j=1}^{M}\left[\frac{\textrm{d}\left[\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(s_{\ell}^{t-1})\right]_{j}}{\textrm{d}\left(s_{\ell}^{t-1}\right)_{j}}\right]^{\top}Q_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}}^{t-1},\quad\text{for}\;i\in[n],

(94)

where
Q^{t}_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}={\tau_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}/{\phi_{\textsf{r}}^{t}}
.

State evolution (SE)

As we show in Theorem
3
below, for
t\geq 1,
in the limit as
L,n\to\infty
with
L/n\to\mu
,
the empirical distribution of the rows of
\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}
in the block
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
converges to a Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{k}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t})
. Similarly, the empirical distribution of the rows of
(\boldsymbol{S}^{t}-\boldsymbol{X})
in block
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
converges to a Gaussian
\mathcal{N}_{k}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})
.
To initialize the state evolution, we make the following assumption
(A0)
on the AMP initialization
\boldsymbol{X}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k}
.

(A0)
Denoting by
\boldsymbol{X}_{\textsf{c}},\,\boldsymbol{X}^{0}_{\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L/\textsf{C}\times k}
the
c
th block of rows of
\boldsymbol{X},\,\boldsymbol{X}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k}
,
we assume that there exists a symmetric non-negative definite matrix
\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
for each
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
such that we almost surely have

\lim_{L\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\textsf{c}}^{0}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\textsf{c}}\right)^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\textsf{c}}^{0}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\textsf{c}}\right)=\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}.

(95)

The AMP initialization
\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{0}=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right]=\boldsymbol{0}_{k}
for
\ell\in[L]
corresponds to
\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}=\text{Cov}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right]=\alpha E_{b}\boldsymbol{I}_{k\times k}
.
The state evolution iteration is initialized with
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{0}
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
given by:

\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{0}=\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}

(96)

The covariance matrices
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
are then iteratively defined for
t\geq 0
via the following state evolution recursion (SE), for
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}=\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}+k\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{c=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\,,

(97)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\left[\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)-\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right]\left[\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)-\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right]^{\top}\right\},

(98)

\displaystyle\text{where }\boldsymbol{G}^{t}_{\textsf{c}}\sim\mathcal{N}_{k}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\,\perp\,\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}},\quad\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}={\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r},\textsf{c}}{[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}]}^{-1}\right]^{-1}}.

(99)

We can interpret
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
as the covariance of the asymptotic estimation error between the estimate
\boldsymbol{X}^{t}
and the true signal matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
, for the
c
-th block of users.

Choice of denoiser
\eta_{t}
in AMP

Since the empirical distribution of the rows of
\boldsymbol{S}^{t}
in block
c
converges to the law of
\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
for
t\geq 1
, the Bayes-optimal denoiser
\eta^{\textrm{Bayes}}_{t,\textsf{c}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
takes the form
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}|\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}=\boldsymbol{s}\right]
.
Although this denoiser minimizes the MSE of the estimate, its computational cost is exponential in
k
, making it impractical for even moderately large payloads.
The marginal-MMSE denoiser
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
with
\big[\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}(\boldsymbol{s})\big]_{j}=\mathbb{E}[\bar{X}_{j}\mid\bar{X}_{j}+(G^{t}_{\textsf{c}})_{j}=s_{j}]
for
j\in[k]
enjoys a computational cost linear in
k
, but performs poorly as it doesn’t account for the correlation between the entries of
\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
. This correlation is due to the point mass at
\boldsymbol{0}
in the prior (
78
), corresponding to the silent users.

In light of the limitations of
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
and
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}
, we propose the following
thresholding
denoiser, denoted by
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{thres}}(\boldsymbol{s}):{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
.
This denoiser first performs a Bayesian hypothesis test using
Y=\|\boldsymbol{s}\|_{2}^{2}/k\in{\mathbb{R}}
as the test statistic, testing
H_{0}:\boldsymbol{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
versus
H_{1}:\boldsymbol{s}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\bar{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
, with prior probabilities
\mathbb{P}(H_{1})=1-\mathbb{P}(H_{0})=\alpha
. If
H_{0}
is chosen, it returns the all-zero vector; if
H_{1}
is chosen, it produces an entrywise MMSE nonzero signal estimate.
Mathematically, we have for
t\geq 1
,
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
j\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\left[\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{thres}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right]_{j}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $H_{0}$ is chosen}\,,\\
\mathbb{E}\big[(\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}\ |\ (\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}+(G_{\textsf{c}}^{t})_{j}=s_{j}\big]&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

(100)

where, since
p\big((\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}=\sqrt{E_{b}}\big)=p\big((\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}=-\sqrt{E_{b}}\big)=\frac{1}{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[(\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}\ |\ (\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}+(G_{\textsf{c}}^{t})_{j}=s_{j}\big]=\sqrt{E_{b}}\tanh\big(\sqrt{E_{b}}s_{j}/(T_{\textsf{c}}^{t})_{j,j}\big).

This denoiser
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{thres}}
has the same
\mathcal{O}(k)
computational cost as
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{marginal}}
, but can noticeably enhance decoding performance because it uses the fact that approximately
(1-\alpha)
fraction of the rows of the signal matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
are all-zero.
The Jacobian of this denoising function can also be easily computed.

Designing the hypothesis test:
We use
\chi_{k}^{2}(\mu)
to denote a chi-squared random variable with
k
degrees of freedom and non-centrality parameter
\mu
. If
\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{k}
are the means of
k
independent Gaussians with unit variance whose squares are summed to form the chi-squared variable, then
\mu=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\mu_{j}^{2}
.
We approximate the entries of
\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
as i.i.d. with variance
\bar{T}^{t}_{\textsf{c}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{k}\sum_{j=1}^{k}(T^{t}_{\textsf{c}})_{j,j}
. Based on this, the test statistic
Y
follows a chi-squared distribution whose non-centrality parameter depends on whether
\boldsymbol{s}
contains a non-zero signal. Furthermore, by the central limit theorem, as
k\to\infty
, we have
\left(\chi_{k}^{2}(\mu)-(k+\mu)\right)/\sqrt{2(k+2\mu)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\to}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Therefore, assuming large
k
, we use the following approximations for the test statistic:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{under }H_{0}:Y\sim(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}/k)\,\chi_{k}^{2}(0)\;\approx\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\,,\,2(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})^{2}/k\right)\,,\\
&\text{under }H_{1}:Y\sim(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}/k)\,\chi_{k}^{2}(kE_{b}/\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\;\approx\mathcal{N}\left(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}+E_{b}\,,\,2\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}+2E_{b})/k\right)\,.\end{split}

(101)

Applying (
101
), the MAP decision rule chooses
H_{0}
if

\displaystyle Y^{2}-\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}Y+\frac{1}{2E_{b}}\left(-\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}E_{b}^{2}-\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}+2E_{b})\cdot\frac{4\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}{k}\ln\left[\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha}\sqrt{\frac{\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}+2E_{b}}{\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}}}\right]\right)<0\,,

(102)

and chooses
H_{1}
otherwise. This implies that
\boldsymbol{s}
is always declared to contain a non-zero signal when
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},1]
with
\alpha^{*}\coloneqq\left[\sqrt{\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}/(\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}+2E_{b})}\exp\left(-E_{b}k/(8\bar{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\right)+1\right]^{-1}
. When
\alpha\in[0,\alpha^{*})
, the decision follows the threshold test in (
102
).
Moreover, depending on the application, one might want to control
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
more strictly than
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
, or vice-versa.
In such cases, a principled approach is to apply the threshold test in (
102
) by substituting a higher value
\hat{\alpha}
for
\alpha
to control
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
more strictly, or a lower value
\hat{\alpha}
to control
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
more strictly. The effectiveness of this approach is illustrated in Fig.
11
in Section
4.4
.

Hard-decision step in AMP

In each iteration
t\geq 1
, the decoder can produce a hard-decision estimate for each row of the signal matrix. The MAP estimate is analogous to the one in (
51
).
Additionally, we also consider a hard-decision estimator complementary to the thresholding denoiser
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}^{\textrm{thres}}
defined in (
100
), which provides an entrywise estimate as follows. For
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
j\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\left[h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})\right]_{j}=\begin{cases}0\qquad\text{if $H_{0}$ is chosen}\,,\\
\argmax_{x\in\{\pm\sqrt{E_{b}}\}}\mathbb{P}\big((\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}=x\ |\ (\bar{X}_{\mathrm{a}})_{j}+(G_{\textsf{c}}^{t})_{j}=s_{j}\big)=\text{sign}(s_{j})\sqrt{E_{b}}\qquad\text{otherwise}\,.\end{cases}

(103)

This estimator has an
\mathcal{O}(k)
computational complexity.

4.3
Asymptotic Error Analysis for AMP Decoding

We first present a general state evolution result, Theorem
3
, that characterizes the asymptotic performance of AMP for the model (
79
) with a spatially coupled design matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
. Theorem
3
applies to generic row-wise priors on the signal
\boldsymbol{X}
and to any Lipschitz denoisers for the AMP algorithm.
It states that the row-wise
empirical distributions of the AMP iterates in (
81
)–(
82
) converge to the laws of the state evolution random variables defined in (
97
)–(
99
). Based on this result, we then obtain Theorem
4
, which characterizes the asymptotic
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
of the AMP decoder for the CDMA scheme.

To present Theorem
3
, we require the following assumptions on the general linear model
\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
, where
\boldsymbol{A}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times L}
,
\boldsymbol{X}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k}
.

(A1)

As
L,\tilde{n}\to\infty
,
L/\tilde{n}\to k\mu
, for some constant
\mu>0
. The number of columns in the signal matrix,
k
, is a fixed constant that does not grow with
n
.

(A2)
Both the signal matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
and noise matrix
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
are independent of
\boldsymbol{A}
.

(A3)
As
n\to\infty
, the row-wise empirical distributions of the signal and the noise matrices converge in Wasserstein distance to well-defined limits. More precisely, write
\nu_{L}(\boldsymbol{X})
and
\nu_{\tilde{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}})
for the row-wise empirical distributions of
\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
, respectively. Then for some
m\in[2,\infty)
, there exist
k
-dimensional random variables
\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}
and
\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}}
with
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{m}dp_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}(\boldsymbol{x}),\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{m}dp_{\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}}(\boldsymbol{x})<\infty
such that
d_{m}(\nu_{L}(\boldsymbol{X}),p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}})\rightarrow 0
and
d_{m}(\nu_{\tilde{n}}(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}),p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}})\rightarrow 0
almost surely. Here
d_{m}(P,Q)
denotes the
m
-Wasserstein distance between distributions
P
and
Q
defined on the same Euclidean probability space.

Theorem
3
is stated in terms of
pseudo-Lipschitz
test functions. A function
\xi:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
is pseudo-Lipschitz of order
m
if
|\xi(\boldsymbol{x})-\xi(\boldsymbol{y})|\leq C\left(1+\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{2}^{m-1}+\|\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}^{m-1}\right)\|\boldsymbol{x}-\boldsymbol{y}\|_{2}
for all
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
, for some constant
C>0
. Pseudo-Lipschitz functions of order
m=2
include the squared difference
\xi(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\|\boldsymbol{u}-\boldsymbol{v}\|_{2}^{2}
, and the correlation
\xi(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})=\left\langle\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}\right\rangle
.

Theorem 3

(State evolution result)
.

Consider the linear model
\boldsymbol{Y}=\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}\,\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k}
with the spatially coupled design matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
in (
80
),
and the AMP decoding algorithm in (
81
)–(
82
) with
a sequence of Lipschitz continuous denoisers
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.
Assume
(A0)
–
(A3)
hold.
Let
\xi:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\zeta:{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be pseudo-Lipschitz test functions of order
m
, where
m
is specified by
(A3)
.
Then, for
t\geq 1
and
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, we almost surely have:

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\xi\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t+1}\,,\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\left(\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right),\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right)\right],

(104)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tilde{n}\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}/\textsf{R}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}}\zeta\left(\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t}\,,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\right)=\mathbb{E}\left[\zeta\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\,,\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\right)\right],

(105)

where
{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{k}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\sim\mathcal{N}_{k}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\right)
with
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
given by (
97
)–(
99
), and

\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}:=\left\{(\textsf{c}-1){L}/{\textsf{C}}+1,\dots,\textsf{c}{L}/{\textsf{C}}\right\}
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}:=\left\{(\textsf{r}-1)\tilde{n}/\textsf{R}+1,\dots,\textsf{r}\tilde{n}/\textsf{R}\right\}
for
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
.

Proof.

The proof of Theorem
3
is given in Section
6
.
∎

Using Theorem
3
, we can obtain Theorem
4
which characterizes
the asymptotic
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
of the AMP decoder (
81
)–(
82
) with a generic Lipschitz denoiser.

Theorem 4

(Asymptotic error probabilities of CDMA scheme)
.

Consider the CDMA-type scheme defined in (
79
) with the spatially coupled design matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
in (
80
), with the rows of the signal matrix
\boldsymbol{X}
drawn i.i.d. according to the prior
p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}
in (
78
).
Consider the AMP decoder (
81
)–(
82
) with a sequence of Lipschitz continuous denoisers
\{\eta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
taking the form of (
4.2
).
Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}^{t+1}=h_{t}(\boldsymbol{S}^{t})
be the hard-decision estimate in iteration
t
.
In the limit as
L,n\to\infty
with
L/n\to\mu
,
the
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
,
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
in iteration
t\geq 1
satisfy the following almost surely:

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}p_{\mathrm{MD}}=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]:\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbbm{1}\{\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\ell}^{t+1}=\boldsymbol{0}\}\Bigg]=\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)=\boldsymbol{0}\right),

(106)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}p_{\mathrm{FA}}=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\{{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}\neq 0\}}{\widehat{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in[L]:\\
\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\ell}^{t+1}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\end{subarray}}\mathbbm{1}\{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}=\boldsymbol{0}\}\Bigg]=\left[\frac{\alpha\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})\neq\boldsymbol{0})}{(1-\alpha)\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}(h_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{G}^{t}_{\textsf{c}})\neq\boldsymbol{0})}+1\right]^{-1},

(107)

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}p_{\mathrm{AUE}}=\lim_{L\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\frac{\mathbbm{1}\{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}\neq 0\}}{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}\sum_{\ell\in[L]:\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\neq\boldsymbol{0}}\mathbbm{1}\big\{\hat{\boldsymbol{X}}_{\ell}^{t+1}\notin\{\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{0}\}\big\}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\quad\quad\qquad\;\;=\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\mathbb{P}\left(h_{t,\textsf{c}}\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\right)\notin\{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}},\boldsymbol{0}\}\right).

(108)

Here
\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}_{\mathrm{a}}}
and
\boldsymbol{G}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\sim\mathcal{N}_{k}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})
are independent, with their laws given in (
78
) and (
99
).

Proof.

We use Theorem
3
to prove Theorem
4
. It is easy to check that assumptions
(A0)
–
(A3)
are satisfied. Setting
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\boldsymbol{s}
in (
104
) gives

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\xi\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{\ell}^{t}\,,\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a.s.}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\left[\xi\left(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{c}}^{t},\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right)\right],

(109)

for any pseudo-Lipschitz function of order
2
.
The results (
106
)–(
108
) then follow from arguments similar to (
70
)–(
71
) in the proof of Theorem
2
.
∎

Corollary 3
.

The asymptotic error guarantees in Theorem
4
hold for the AMP decoder with any of the three denoisers:
\eta^{\textrm{Bayes}}_{t,\textsf{c}},\eta^{\textrm{marginal}}_{t,\textsf{c}}
, and for
\eta^{\textrm{thres}}_{t,\textsf{c}}
.

Proof.

All three denoisers can be verified to be Lipschitz continuous by direct differentiation. The result then follows from Theorem
4
.
∎

We remark that in the special case of an i.i.d. Gaussian design matrix (i.e.,
\textsf{R}=\textsf{C}=1
), the asymptotic characterization of the CDMA scheme in Theorem
4
can be directly obtained from the state evolution result of Bayati and Montanari
[
21
]
.

4.4
Numerical Results

The Python code for all simulations presented in this section can be found at
[
54
]
.
Fig.
10
plots
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
, and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
for the CDMA scheme, with a user payload of
k=60
bits. For such payloads, the marginal-MMSE denoiser
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{marginal}}
and the thresholding denoiser
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{thres}}
are the only computationally viable options,
coupled with the entrywise MAP hard-decision step or the thresholding hard-decision step defined in (
103
). The two subfigures of Fig.
10
compare the performance of the two denoisers for varying
E_{b}/N_{0}
. We observe that the thresholding
denoiser
\eta^{\textrm{thres}}_{t}
achieves significantly lower error probabilities. In both cases, the empirical error probabilities (crosses) closely match the asymptotic formulas from Theorem
4
(solid lines).

Fig.
11
shows the limiting error probabilities for the CDMA scheme using the thresholding denoiser
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{thres}}
, where the threshold test (
102
) uses a user-chosen value
\hat{\alpha}
for
\alpha
instead of the true
\alpha=0.7
. The curves are obtained using state evolution and the expressions in Theorem
4
. We observe that the denoiser balances
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
equally when
\hat{\alpha}=\alpha
(Fig.
11b
). However, when
\hat{\alpha}\neq\alpha
, the performance becomes asymmetric: choosing
\hat{\alpha}<\alpha
biases the denoiser towards detecting silent users, favoring a smaller
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
(Fig.
11a
), while
choosing
\hat{\alpha}>\alpha
biases the denoiser towards detecting active users, leading to a smaller
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
(Fig.
11c
).

Additionally, we observe that the CDMA schemes tend to incur higher
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
than
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
or
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
, which contrasts the random coding schemes illustrated in Figs.
1
and
7
where
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
is typically
lower
than
p_{\mathrm{MD}}
and
p_{\mathrm{FA}}
. This is because, in the CDMA schemes, the AMP decoder recovers each user’s message as a sequence of
k
bipolar symbols or all zeros, and it is more likely to make a symbol error than to mistake all
k
symbols for zeros, or vice versa. In contrast, for random coding, the maximum-likelihood decoder in (
7
) or the AMP decoder in (
35
)–(
36
) is more likely to mistake a transmitted codeword for noise, or vice versa, than to mistake a transmitted codeword for a different codeword.

p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}},p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
and

\max\{p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+p_{\mathrm{AUE}}

for the CDMA scheme with
k=60,\alpha=0.7,\mu_{\mathrm{a}}=0.013
,

, and spatial coupling parameters

.

, where

, and spatial coupling parameters are

. The denoiser
\eta^{\textrm{thres}}
uses the threshold test (
102
) with a mismatched value
\hat{\alpha}
for
\alpha
.

compares the limiting error probabilities of the efficient CDMA scheme in Theorem
4
with the two achievability bounds from Theorems
1
and

. We plot the maximum active user density
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
achievable as a function of
E_{b}/N_{0}
for a

\max\{p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+p_{\mathrm{AUE}}\leq 0.01
.

The asymptotic achievability bounds from Theorem

are computed using either the potential function
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
(dashed black) or
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
(solid black) with
\Lambda\to\infty
then
\omega\to\infty
.

Fig.
12a
considers a small user payload
k=6
, same setting as in Figs.
6
–
7
. The CDMA schemes employ AMP decoding with either the Bayes-optimal denoiser
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{Bayes}}
(solid blue) or the thresholding denoiser
\eta_{t}^{\textrm{thres}}
(solid orange). Notably, the achievable region of the efficient thresholding denoiser is only slightly smaller than that of the Bayes-optimal denoiser, and the latter is smaller than that of random coding (solid and dashed black).

The dotted black curve in Fig.
12a
is computed using the finite-length achievability bounds from Theorem
1
with
L=100

and
({\kappa_{l}},{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}})
chosen so that
\mathbb{P}({K_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[{\kappa_{l}}:{\kappa_{\mathrm{u}}}])\leq 10^{-6}
.
The decoding
radii
are chosen to be
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
, which implies that the maximum-likelihood decoder in (
7
) searches through all possible combinations of active users out of
L
,
and
P^{\prime}
is optimized over the full range of values
(0,P)
.
This gives the tightest achievability bound in this setting.
We observe that the overall shape of all the plots in Fig.
12a
is quite similar. They are steep (near-vertical) for very low active user densities (
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}<0.17
), where the multi-user interference is nearly cancelled, essentially achieving the performance of a single-user channel. The plots exhibit a sharp transition at around
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}\in(0.17,0.21)
. Beyond this point, the plots display a much shallower slope due to the noticeable multi-user interference.
This observation is consistent with the findings in
[
3
,
4
,
6
,
55
]
.

(a)
k=6
, CDMA scheme uses
\omega=6,\Lambda=40
.

(b)
k=60
, CDMA scheme uses
\omega=11,\Lambda=50
.

Achievable
\mu_{\mathrm{a}}
as a function of
E_{b}/N_{0}
for a target
total error
\max\{p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}\}+p_{\mathrm{AUE}}\leq 0.01
with

\alpha=0.7
,
p_{{K_{\mathrm{a}}}}=\text{Bin}(\alpha,L)
, and

or
