) yields (
148
), which completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
9
.

Proof of part
1
:

Given
{K_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}
,
{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}
,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{e}}
(with
|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{e}}|=t
),
|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{e}}|={\hat{t}}
,
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}
, the elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{e}}
are fully determined by the remaining subsets
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
. Therefore, counting the number of distinct configurations of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{e}}
reduces to counting the distinct ways to construct
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
.

To begin,
note that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}

does not
contain codewords from the codebooks of the users:

•

who transmitted
\mathcal{W}\cap\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
since these are correctly decoded,

•

who transmitted
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{iMD}}
or
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}
because these users are declared silent, or

•

who are declared active via the
\mathtt{FA}
s in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}
or
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}
.

As a result,
the elements of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
exclusively come from the remaining
\mathcal{R}
codebooks, with

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle=L-|\mathcal{W}\cap\widehat{\mathcal{W}}|-|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{iMD}}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}|-|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}|

\displaystyle=L-\big({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}|\big)-|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}|

\displaystyle=L-({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-{t_{\min}})-\big((\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}+({\hat{t}}-t)^{+}\big)

\displaystyle=L-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}+{t_{\min}}-(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}-({\hat{t}}-t)^{+}\ .

(158)

These
\mathcal{R}
codebooks include

1.

the codebooks of the
|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}|={t_{\min}}
users who transmitted
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}\cup\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
, or

2.

the codebooks of the
(\mathcal{R}-{t_{\min}})
silent users, who are
not
mistakenly declared active through
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}
.

As a sanity check on
2
, there are
(L-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})
silent users in total, among which

|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}|+|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}|=(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}+({\hat{t}}-t)^{+}
are mistaken as active, so the number of users in the category
2
is

(L-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})-\big((\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})^{+}+({\hat{t}}-t)^{+}\big)
, which is equal to
(\mathcal{R}-{t_{\min}})
due to (
158
).

Observe that each element of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}
comes from a type-
2
codebook, and each element of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
comes from the set of untransmitted codewords in a type-
1
codebook. The number of ways to divide the set
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
into
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
such that
|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}|=\psi
and
|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}|={t_{\min}}-\psi
is

\displaystyle\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)=\binom{\mathcal{R}-{t_{\min}}}{\psi}M^{\psi}\cdot\binom{{t_{\min}}}{{t_{\min}}-\psi}(M-1)^{{t_{\min}}-\psi}.

Thus the total number of ways to divide
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}\cup\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
into
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eAUE}}
is

\displaystyle\nu({t_{\min}})=\sum_{\psi=0}^{\min\{{t_{\min}},\mathcal{R}-{t_{\min}}\}}\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)\leq\binom{\mathcal{R}}{{t_{\min}}}M^{t_{\min}}\,.

Proof of part
2
:

Recall that
|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}|
as illustrated in Fig.
14
. We characterize the conditional distribution of
|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}|

by characterizing the conditional distribution of
|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}^{1}|
.
Combining
\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)
and
\nu({t_{\min}})
and using the exchangeability of the codewords yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}|=\psi\,\bigg|\,{K_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}},|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{e}}|=t,|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{e}}|={\hat{t}},\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{e}},\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}\right)=\nu({t_{\min}},\psi)/\nu({t_{\min}}).

(159)

Taking expectation over
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{e}},\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{eFA}}
yields
(
147
).

Note that (
146
)–(
147
) imply that

\mathbb{P}\big(\left\lvert\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{eMD}}^{1}\right\rvert=\psi\,\big|\,{K_{\mathrm{a}}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}},{K_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}={\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}},\left\lvert\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{e}}\right\rvert=t,|\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{e}}|={\hat{t}}\big)=1
when
\psi=0
and
{t_{\min}}=\min\{t,{\hat{t}}\}=0
, as one would expect.
∎

5.4
Proof of Corollary
1

When
E_{b}/N_{0}\to\infty
, the maximum-likelihood decoder doesn’t make mistakes as long as
{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}\in[\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}:\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}]
. Under the new measure defined in Section
5.1
,
{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}\in[\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}:\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}]
can be ensured by choosing
{r_{l}}={r_{\mathrm{u}}}=L
.
However, given fixed
{r_{l}}
and
{r_{\mathrm{u}}}
that do not scale with
L
, it is possible that
{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}\notin[\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}:\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}]
. When this happens, we have one of the following two scenarios:

•

if
\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}>{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}
, then
\widehat{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}\geq\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}>{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}
, hence the decoder commits at least
(\underline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}-{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}})

\mathtt{FA}
s in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}
, and guarantees that all transmitted codewords are decoded correctly, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}\subset\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
;

•

if
\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}<{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}
, then
\widehat{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}\leq\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}}<{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}
, hence the decoder commits at least
({\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}}-\overline{\kappa_{\mathrm{a}}^{\prime}})

\mathtt{MD}
s in
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{iMD}}
, and guarantees that the decoded set only contains transmitted codewords, i.e.,
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}\subset\mathcal{W}
.

Consequently, as
{E_{b}}/{N_{0}}\to\infty
(or
P\to\infty
), the error floors are calculated assuming no additional errors are incurred on top of the initial errors
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{iMD}}
or
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\mathrm{iFA}}
(i.e.,
t={\hat{t}}=0
). Taking
t={\hat{t}}=0
in (
8
)–(
10
) and noticing that
\lim_{P^{\prime}\to\infty}\lim_{P\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\|\boldsymbol{c}_{w_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\|_{2}^{2}>nP)=0
in (
112
), we obtain (
26
)–(
28
) in Corollary
1
. ∎

6
Proof of Theorem
3

We first describe in Section
6.1
an abstract AMP iteration with matrix-valued iterates. The abstract AMP iteration, defined for an i.i.d. Gaussian matrix, does not correspond to a specific signal/observation model. The state evolution result for this abstract AMP was established in
[
57
]
; see also
[
31
, Sec. 6.7]
and
[
58
]
. We use this result to prove Theorem
3
, by showing that the spatially coupled AMP in (
81
)–(
82
) for the linear model in (
79
) is an instance of the abstract AMP iteration with matrix-valued iterates.

6.1
Abstract AMP recursion for i.i.d. Gaussian matrices

The abstract AMP and the state evolution result in this subsection is similar to the one in
[
36
, Section V.A]
, but we present it here for completeness.

Let
\boldsymbol{M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times L}
be a random matrix with entries
M_{i\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,{1}/{\tilde{n}})
. Consider the following recursion with iterates
\boldsymbol{e}^{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times\ell_{E}}
and
\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times\ell_{H}}
, defined for
t\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{M}^{\top}g_{t}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t},\,\boldsymbol{\gamma})\,-\,f_{t}(\boldsymbol{h}^{t},\,\boldsymbol{\beta})\textsf{D}^{\top}_{t}\,,

(160)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{M}f_{t+1}(\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1},\,\boldsymbol{\beta})\,-\,g_{t}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t},\,\boldsymbol{\gamma})\textsf{B}_{t+1}^{\top},

(161)

where
\boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k}
are matrices independent of
\boldsymbol{M}
.
The recursion is initialized with some
\boldsymbol{i}^{0}\equiv f_{0}(\boldsymbol{h}^{0},\,\boldsymbol{\beta})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times\ell_{E}}
and
\boldsymbol{e}^{0}=\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{i}^{0}
.
Here, the functions
f_{t+1}:{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times(\ell_{H}+k)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times\ell_{E}}
and
g_{t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times(\ell_{E}+k)}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times\ell_{H}}
are defined component-wise as follows, for
t\geq 0
.
With the sets
[\tilde{n}]
and
[L]
partitioned into
\{\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}\}_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}
and
\{\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}\}_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}
as defined below (
105
),
we assume that there exist functions
\tilde{f}_{t+1}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times[\textsf{C}]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{E}}
and

\tilde{g}_{t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{E}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times[\textsf{R}]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{H}}
such that

\begin{split}&f_{t+1,\ell}(\boldsymbol{h},\boldsymbol{\beta})=\tilde{f}_{t+1}(\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell},\,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell},\ \textsf{c}),\quad\text{ for }\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}},\quad\boldsymbol{h}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times\ell_{H}},\ \boldsymbol{\beta}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k},\\
&g_{t,i}(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{\gamma})=\tilde{g}_{t}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i},\,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i},\ \textsf{r}),\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}},\quad\boldsymbol{e}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times\ell_{E}},\ \boldsymbol{\gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k}.\end{split}

(162)

(Here we note that
\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{e}_{i},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}
are all row vectors.) In words, the functions
f_{t+1},g_{t}
act row-wise on their inputs and depend only on the indices of the column block/row block that their inputs come from.
The matrices
\textsf{D}_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{H}\times\ell_{E}}
and
\textsf{B}_{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{H}}
in (
160
)–(
161
) are defined as:

\begin{split}\textsf{D}_{t}=\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}\tilde{g}_{t}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i},\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i},\textsf{r}),\quad\textsf{B}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\tilde{f}_{t+1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell},\textsf{c}),\end{split}

(163)

where
\tilde{g}_{t}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{f}_{t+1}^{\prime}
denote the Jacobians of
\tilde{g}_{t}
and
\tilde{f}_{t+1}
with respect to their first argument.

Assumptions

(B0)

As the dimensions
n,L\to\infty
, the aspect ratio
\frac{L}{n}\to\mu>0
, and we emphasize that
\ell_{E},\ell_{H}
as well as
\textsf{R},\textsf{C}
are positive integers that do not scale with
n,L
.

(B1)

The functions
\tilde{f}_{t}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\textsf{c})
and

\tilde{g}_{t}(\cdot\,,\cdot\,,\textsf{r})
are Lipschitz for
t\geq 1
,
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
.

(B2)

For
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
, let
\nu(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\textsf{c}})
and
\pi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\textsf{r}})
denote the empirical distributions of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\textsf{c}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell})_{\ell\in[\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}]}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(L/\textsf{C})\times k}
and

\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\textsf{r}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})_{i\in[\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}]}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(\tilde{n}/\textsf{R})\times k}
, respectively. Then, for some
m\in[2,\infty)
, there exist
k
-dimensional vector random variables
\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}}\sim\nu_{\textsf{c}}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}}\sim\pi_{\textsf{r}}
,
with
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{m}d\nu_{\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{x})
,
\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{k}}\|\boldsymbol{x}\|^{m}d\pi_{\textsf{r}}(\boldsymbol{x})<\infty

such that
d_{m}(\nu(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\textsf{c}}),\nu_{\textsf{c}})\rightarrow 0
and
d_{m}(\pi(\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{\textsf{r}}),\pi_{\textsf{r}})\rightarrow 0
almost surely. Here
d_{m}(P,Q)
is the
m
-Wasserstein distance between distributions
P,Q
defined on the same Euclidean probability space.

(B3)

For
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, we assume there exists a symmetric non-negative definite
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{E}}
such that we almost surely have

\displaystyle k\mu\cdot\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{(L/\textsf{C})}(\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\textsf{c}})^{\top}\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\textsf{c}}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}},

(164)

where
\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\textsf{c}}\equiv(\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\ell})_{\ell\in[\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}]}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{(L/\textsf{C})\times\ell_{E}}
.

Theorem
5
below states that for each
t\geq 1
, the empirical distribution of the rows of
\boldsymbol{e}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times\ell_{E}}
converge to the law of a Gaussian random vector
\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\ell_{E}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t})
. Similarly, the empirical distribution of the rows of
\boldsymbol{h}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times\ell_{H}}
converge to the law of a Gaussian random vector
\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{\ell_{H}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t})
. Here the covariance matrices
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}
and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t}
are defined by the state evolution recursion, described below.

State evolution

The state evolution is initialized with
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}=\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}
, where
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}
is defined in (
164
). Then for
t\geq 1
, we recursively compute
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{H}\times\ell_{H}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{E}\times\ell_{E}}
given
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}
as follows:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}=\frac{1}{\textsf{R}}\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t+1,\textsf{r}}\,,

(165)

where for
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
,

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t+1,\textsf{r}}=\mathbb{E}\left\{\tilde{g}_{t}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t},\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}},\,\textsf{r})\,\tilde{g}_{t}(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t},\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}},\,\textsf{r})^{\top}\right\},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t})\,\perp\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}}\sim\pi_{\textsf{r}}.

(166)

Similarly,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}=\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t+1,\textsf{c}}\,,

(167)

where for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
,

\displaystyle\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t+1,\textsf{c}}=k\mu\cdot\mathbb{E}\left\{\tilde{f}_{t+1}({\boldsymbol{U}}^{t+1},\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}},\,\textsf{c})\,\tilde{f}_{t+1}({\boldsymbol{U}}^{t+1},\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}},\,\textsf{c})^{\top}\right\},\quad{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t+1}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1})\,\perp\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}}\sim\nu_{\textsf{c}}.

(168)

The random variables
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t},\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}},\,{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t+1},\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}}
are treated as column vectors in (
166
) and (
168
), and
k\mu=L/\tilde{n}
.

Theorem 5
.

Consider the abstract AMP recursion in (
160
)–(
161
) under the Assumptions
(B0)
–
(B3)
above. Let
\xi:{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{H}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and
\zeta:{\mathbb{R}}^{\ell_{E}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be any pseudo-Lipschitz functions of order
m
, where
m
is specified in Assumption
(B2)
. Then, for
t\geq 1
,
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, we almost surely have:

\displaystyle\lim_{L\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\xi(\boldsymbol{h}^{t}_{\ell}\,,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{\ell})=\mathbb{E}\{\xi({\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\,,\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}})\},\quad{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t})\,\perp\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{\textsf{c}}\sim\nu_{\textsf{c}}\,,

(169)

\displaystyle\lim_{\tilde{n}\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{\tilde{n}/\textsf{R}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}}\zeta(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i}\,,\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})=\mathbb{E}\{\zeta(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\,,\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}})\},\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t})\,\perp\,\bar{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{\textsf{r}}\sim\pi_{\textsf{r}}\,.

(170)

Theorem
5
can be proved by applying
[
57
, Thm. 1]
, which gives an analogous state evolution result for an AMP recursion defined via a
symmetric
Gaussian matrix; see
[
36
, Section V.A]
.

6.2
Reduction of Spatially Coupled Matrix AMP to Abstract AMP

We define the i.i.d. Gaussian matrix
\boldsymbol{M}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times L}
in terms of the spatially coupled matrix
\boldsymbol{A}
in (
80
) as follows.
For
i\in[\tilde{n}],\,\ell\in[L]
,

M_{i\ell}=\begin{cases}A_{i\ell}/\sqrt{\textsf{R}\,W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}(\ell)}},&\quad\text{ if }W_{\textsf{r}(i),\textsf{c}(\ell)}\neq 0,\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{indep.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,1/{\tilde{n}}),&\quad\text{ otherwise }.\end{cases}

(171)

From (
80
), we have that
M_{i\ell}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,{1}/{\tilde{n}})
for
i\in[\tilde{n}],\,\ell\in[L]
. Using the matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
, we define an abstract AMP recursion for the form in (
160
)–(
162
), with iterates

\boldsymbol{e}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k\textsf{R}}
and
\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k\textsf{C}}
. This is done via the following choice of functions
\tilde{f}_{t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times[\textsf{C}]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}
and

\tilde{g}_{t}:{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\times[\textsf{R}]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}}
, for
t\geq 0
. In (
162
), we take
\boldsymbol{\beta}:=\boldsymbol{X}
and
\boldsymbol{\gamma}:=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}
, and let

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{t}(\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell},\,\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\ \textsf{c})=\sqrt{\textsf{R}}\,\left[\left(\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}})-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)^{\top}\,[\sqrt{W_{1\textsf{c}}},\ldots,\sqrt{W_{\textsf{R}\textsf{c}}}]\right]^{\top}\quad\text{for }\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}},\ \boldsymbol{h}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}},

(172)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{t}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i},\,\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\ \textsf{r})=\sqrt{\textsf{R}}\,\left[\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{i,\textsf{r}}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\right)^{\top}\,[\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}_{\textsf{r}1}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}1}},\,\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}_{\textsf{r}2}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}2}},\ldots,\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{C}}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{C}}}]\right]^{\top}\quad\text{for }i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}},\ \boldsymbol{e}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}.

(173)

Here
\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell}=[\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,1},\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{C}}]
and
\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
denotes the
c
th length-
k
section of the vector
\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}}
. Similarly,
\boldsymbol{e}_{i,\textsf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
denotes the
r
th length-
k
section of the vector
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}
.
We note that the vectors in (
172
)–(
173
) are treated as column vectors even though they represent the
j
th row of the functions
f_{t},g_{t}
, as given in (
162
).

\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
is the (
\textsf{r},\textsf{c}
)th
k\times k
submatrix of
\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}\times k\textsf{C}}
defined in (
90
).

Consider the AMP algorithm (
160
)–(
161
) defined via the matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
in (
171
), the functions in (
172
)–(
173
), and the matrices
\textsf{B}_{t},\textsf{D}_{t}
. The algorithm is initialized with
\boldsymbol{i}^{0}\equiv f_{0}(\boldsymbol{h}^{0},\,\boldsymbol{\beta})
, whose
\ell
th row is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\ell}=\sqrt{\textsf{R}}\,\left[(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{0}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell})^{\top}\,\left[\sqrt{W_{1\textsf{c}}},\ldots,\sqrt{W_{\textsf{R}\textsf{c}}}\right]\right]^{\top},\quad\text{for }\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}},\ \boldsymbol{i}_{\ell}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}.

(174)

We set
\boldsymbol{e}^{0}=\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{i}^{0}
.

With the choice of functions above, the state evolution recursion (
165
)–(
168
) reduces to the following. Given
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}\times k\textsf{R}}
and
\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}\times k\textsf{C}}
, the
(\textsf{c},\textsf{c}^{\prime})
th
k\times k
submatrix of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t+1,\textsf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}\times k\textsf{C}}
for
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
takes the form

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t+1,\textsf{r}}\right]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\textsf{R}\,\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}{}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}-\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\right)\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}-\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\right)^{\top}\right\}\boldsymbol{Q}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}^{\prime}}\,\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}^{\prime}}},\quad\text{for}\quad\textsf{c},\,\textsf{c}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{C}].

(175)

Here
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
denotes the
r
th length-
k
section of
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}
with
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t})\perp\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}}
.
Both
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}
and
\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}
are treated as column vectors.
Similarly, the
(\textsf{r},\textsf{r}^{\prime})
th
k\times k
submatrix of
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t+1,\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}\times k\textsf{R}}
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
takes the form

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t+1,\textsf{c}}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}^{\prime}}=k\mu\,\textsf{R}\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(\eta_{t+1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t+1}_{\textsf{c}})-\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right)\left(\eta_{t+1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t+1}_{\textsf{c}})-\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right)^{\top}\vphantom{{}^{\top}}\right\}

\displaystyle\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}}\,,

\displaystyle\text{for}\quad\textsf{r},\,\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}].

(176)

Here
{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{c}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
denotes the
c
th length-
k
section of
{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}}
with
{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t})\perp\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\sim p_{\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}}
.
The state evolution is initialized with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}=k\mu\lim_{L\to\infty}\frac{1}{(L/\textsf{C})}(\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\textsf{c}})^{\textsf{T}}\boldsymbol{i}^{0}_{\textsf{c}}
, for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
, with
\boldsymbol{i}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{L\times k\textsf{R}}
defined in (
174
). Using Assumption
(A0)
(see (
95
)), its entries are given by

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}^{\prime}}=k\mu\textsf{R}\,\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}}\,,\quad\textsf{r}\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}].

(177)

We then have
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}=\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}
.

To prove Theorem
3
, we show that for
t\geq 0
and
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
,
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
:

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}\,=\,k\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1},\quad\text{and}\quad\left[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}\right]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}}\,=\,\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}{\left[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\right]}^{-1}\right]^{-1},

(178)

where the right side of the equalities consists of the state evolution parameters defined in (
97
)–(
99
). This implies that
\boldsymbol{U}_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}+\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}
for
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
, where
{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}_{d}(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t})
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\sim\mathcal{N}_{d}\left(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\right)
, with
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}
and
\boldsymbol{T}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
defined in (
97
) and (
99
).
We then show that, for
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i,\textsf{r}}=-\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}},\ \textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}],

(179)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{S}^{t}_{\ell},\quad\text{ for }\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}},\ \textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}].

(180)

Here
\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t}
denote the
i
th and
\ell
th rows, respectively, of the spatially coupled AMP iterates
\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}
and
\boldsymbol{X}^{t}
. Theorem
3
follows by substituting (
178
)–(
180
) into Theorem
5
, the state evolution result for the abstract AMP.

We now prove (
178
) and then (
179
)–(
180
).

Proof of (
178
)

For
t=0
, from (
177
) we have

\begin{split}\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{0,\textsf{c}}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}=k\mu\textsf{R}\,\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}.\end{split}

(181)

Therefore

\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{0}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}=k\mu\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}.

(182)

Noting from (
95
)–(
96
) that
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{c}^{0}=\boldsymbol{\Xi}_{\textsf{c}}
for
\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]
and
\mu_{\text{in}}=\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\mu
, we see that the first claim in (
178
) holds for
t=0
. Assume towards induction that for some
t\geq 0
we have

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}\,=\,k\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t},\quad\text{ and }\quad\left[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t}\right]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}}\,=\,\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}{[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t-1}]}^{-1}\right]^{-1},

(183)

(The second part of the induction hypothesis (
183
) is ignored for
t=0
.) From (
175
) we have:

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}\right]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\textsf{R}}\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Omega}}^{t+1,\textsf{r}}\right]_{\textsf{c}\,\textsf{c}}=\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{R}}\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}{}^{\top}\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}-\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\right)\left(\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}-\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\right)^{\top}\right\}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}},

\displaystyle=\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}\,W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}{}^{\top}\left(\mathbb{E}\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}{}^{\top}\right\}-\mathbb{E}\left\{\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}{}^{\top}\right\}-\mathbb{E}\left\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}}\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}^{\top}\right\}+\mathbb{E}\left\{\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}^{\top}\right\}\right)\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,,

\displaystyle=\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}{}^{\top}\left(\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I}\right)\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,,

(184)

where
\tilde{\boldsymbol{U}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}},\boldsymbol{\bar{\mathcal{E}}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
are treated as column vectors. Recalling from (
97
) and (
90
) that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}=\left[\Sigma^{t}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}+\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{I},\qquad\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}=[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}]^{-1}\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{-1}\right]^{-1},

(185)

the expression for
\left[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}\right]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}}
in (
184
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}\right]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\left(\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{-1}\right]^{-1}\right)^{\top}\left([\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}]^{-1}\right)^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}]^{-1}\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{\ -1}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{\ -1}\right]^{-1}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}]^{-1}\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}]^{-1}\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{\ -1}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{-1}\right]^{-1}\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\left[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}\right]^{-1}\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{-1}\right]^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]}W_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}\textsf{c}}[\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}^{\prime}}^{t}]^{-1}\right]^{-1},

(186)

where we have used the fact that
\boldsymbol{\Phi}_{\textsf{r}}^{t}
is a sum of covariance matrices and is therefore symmetric, as is its inverse. This implies that
\boldsymbol{U}_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm d}}{{=}}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
.

Next, from (
176
) we have that

\displaystyle\left[\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{t+1}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}\,

\displaystyle=\frac{k\mu}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}\left[\hat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{t+1,\textsf{c}}\right]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{r}}

\displaystyle=\frac{k\mu\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\mathbb{E}\left\{\left(\eta_{t+1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+\boldsymbol{U}^{t+1}_{\textsf{c}})-\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right)\left(\eta_{t+1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}+\boldsymbol{U}^{t+1}_{\textsf{c}})-\boldsymbol{\bar{X}}\right)^{\top}\right\}\,

\displaystyle=k\mu_{\text{in}}\sum_{\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}]}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t+1},

(187)

where we recall that
\boldsymbol{U}^{t+1}_{\textsf{c}}\sim{\mathcal{N}}\left(0,[\boldsymbol{\Omega}^{t+1}]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{c}}\right)
and the definition of
\boldsymbol{\Psi}_{\textsf{c}}^{t}
in (
98
).
With the expressions in (
186
) and (
187
), we complete the induction step and hence, prove (
178
).

Proof of (
179
) and (
180
)

At
t=0
, the algorithm is initialized with
\boldsymbol{i}^{0}
defined in (
174
), and
\boldsymbol{e}^{0}=\boldsymbol{M}\boldsymbol{i}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\tilde{n}\times k\textsf{R}}
. For
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
and
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}
, consider the
r
th section of the
i
th row of
\boldsymbol{e}^{0}
,
\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}
. Writing the sum over
\ell
from
1
to
L
as a double sum, we have that:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{0}_{i,\textsf{r}}=\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}M_{i\ell}\sqrt{R\,W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}}\,\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{0}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right).

(188)

Recalling the definition of
M_{i\ell}
in (
171
), we have that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{0}_{i,\textsf{r}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}A_{i\ell}\left(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{0}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)=\left(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}^{0}\right)_{i}-\left(\boldsymbol{A}\boldsymbol{X}\right)_{i}=-\boldsymbol{Z}^{0}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},

(189)

where the last equality follows from the initialization of
\boldsymbol{Z}^{0}
in (
92
).

Assume towards induction that for some
t\geq 0
, we have:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i,\textsf{r}}=-\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\quad\text{ for }i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}},\ \textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}],

(190)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}^{t}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t-1}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t-1},\quad\text{ for }\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}},\ \textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}].

(191)

(For
t=0
, we only assume (
190
), as shown in (
189
).)

From (
160
), the
\ell
th row of
\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}
, for
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}
, is given by

\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell}=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}M_{i\ell}\,\tilde{g}_{t}(\boldsymbol{e}_{i}^{t},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\textsf{r}(i))\,-\,\tilde{f}_{t}(\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell}^{t},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\textsf{c}){\textsf{D}}_{t}^{\top},

(192)

where from (
163
),

\displaystyle\textsf{D}_{t}=\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}\tilde{g}_{t}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\textsf{r}(i))=\frac{1}{\textsf{R}}\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}\frac{1}{\tilde{n}/\textsf{R}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}}\tilde{g}_{t}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\textsf{r})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{C}\times k\textsf{R}}.

(193)

Here
\tilde{g}_{t}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\textsf{r}(i))
denotes the Jacobian with respect to the first argument
\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}}
. Using the definition of
\tilde{g}_{t}
in (
173
), the
k\times k
submatrices of its Jacobian are:

\displaystyle[\tilde{g}_{t}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\,\textsf{r})]_{\mathsf{j}\mathsf{m}}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{R}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\mathsf{j}}}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\mathsf{j}},&\text{ for }\mathsf{j}\in[\textsf{C}],\,\mathsf{m}=\textsf{r},\\
\boldsymbol{0},&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

(194)

Using this, we obtain that
[{\textsf{D}}_{t}]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{r}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
is given by

\displaystyle[{\textsf{D}}_{t}]_{\textsf{c}\textsf{r}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\textsf{R}}}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}^{t},\quad\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}],\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}].

(195)

Using (
195
) in (
192
) along with the definition of
\tilde{f}_{t}
from (
172
), we obtain

\begin{split}&\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}=\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}}M_{i\ell}\sqrt{\textsf{R}}\,\left(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i,\textsf{r}}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\right)\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,-\,\left(\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t})-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)\,\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}^{t}{}^{\top}\end{split}

(196)

By the induction hypothesis, we have that

\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i,\textsf{r}}=-\boldsymbol{Z}^{t}_{i}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
, for
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}
, and
\boldsymbol{h}^{t}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t-1}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t-1}
for
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}
. (For
t=0
, we only assume the hypothesis on
\boldsymbol{e}^{t}
, and the formula in (
196
) holds for
\boldsymbol{h}^{1}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}
with the term
\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t})
replaced by
\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{0}
.)
Moreover, substituting the expression for
M_{i\ell}
in (
171
) and noting from the definition of
\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}^{t}
in (
90
) that
\sum_{\textsf{r}=1}^{\textsf{R}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}^{t}{}^{\top}=\boldsymbol{I}_{d\times d}
we obtain:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}A_{i\ell}\left(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i,\textsf{r}(i)}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\right)\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}(i)\textsf{c}}\,-\,\left(\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t})-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}A_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}(i)\textsf{c}}\,-\,\left(\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t})-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\,-\,\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t})\,-\,\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}A_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}(i)\textsf{c}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\,-\,\eta_{t-1,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t-1}+\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t-1})-\sum_{i=1}^{\tilde{n}}A_{i\ell}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}(i)\textsf{c}}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t}.

(197)

where the last equality follows from the definition of
\boldsymbol{V}^{t}
and
\boldsymbol{X}^{t}
in (
93
) and (
82
).

Next consider the
i
th row of
\boldsymbol{e}^{t+1}
, for
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}
, which from (
161
), is given by

e^{t+1}_{i}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}M_{i\ell}\,\tilde{f}_{t+1}(\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell}^{t+1},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\textsf{c}(\ell))\,-\,\tilde{g}_{t}(\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i},\textsf{r})\,{\textsf{B}}_{t+1}^{\top},

(198)

where from (
163
),

\displaystyle\textsf{B}_{t+1}=\frac{1}{\tilde{n}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}\tilde{f}_{t+1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\textsf{c}(\ell))=k\mu\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}\tilde{f}_{t+1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\textsf{c})\,\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\textsf{R}\times k\textsf{C}}.

(199)

From the definition of
\tilde{f}_{t}
in (
172
), the
k\times k
partitions of the Jacobian
\tilde{f}_{t+1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\textsf{c}(\ell))
for
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{c}
are:

[\tilde{f}_{t+1}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell}^{t+1},\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell},\,\textsf{c})]_{\mathsf{j}\mathsf{m}}=\begin{cases}-\sqrt{\textsf{R}}\sqrt{W_{\mathsf{j}\textsf{c}}}\,\left[\eta^{\prime}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})\right],&\text{ for }\mathsf{j}\in[\textsf{R}],\mathsf{m}=\textsf{c},\\
\boldsymbol{0},&\text{ otherwise},\end{cases}

(200)

where
\left[\eta^{\prime}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})\right]\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k\times k}
represents the Jacobian of
\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle[{\textsf{B}}_{t+1}]_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}=-\frac{1}{\delta}\frac{1}{\textsf{C}}\sqrt{\textsf{R}}\sqrt{W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}}\,\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{c}}\{\eta^{\prime}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})\},

\displaystyle\text{ for }\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}],\textsf{c}\in[\textsf{C}].

(201)

We now use (
201
) in (
198
) along with the definition of
\tilde{g}_{t}
in (
173
). For
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}
,
\textsf{r}\in[\textsf{R}]
, we have:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{t+1}_{i,\textsf{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}}M_{i\ell}\sqrt{R\,W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}}\,\left(\eta_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\boldsymbol{e}_{i,\textsf{r}}^{t}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\right)\,\frac{k\mu\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\left\{\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{c}}\left(\eta^{\prime}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})\right)^{\top}\right\}.

(202)

Next, by the induction hypothesis we have that

\boldsymbol{e}^{t}_{i,\textsf{r}}=-\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t}+\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}
for
i\in\mathcal{I}_{\textsf{r}}
, and we have shown above that
\boldsymbol{h}^{t+1}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}=\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t}-\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t}
for
\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{\textsf{c}}
.
Using these in (
202
) along with
\mu_{\text{in}}=\frac{\textsf{R}}{\textsf{C}}\mu
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}^{t+1}_{i,\textsf{r}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}A_{i\ell}\,\left(\eta_{t,\textsf{c}(\ell)}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t}+\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t})-\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}\right)\,-\,k\mu_{\text{in}}\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t}\,\sum_{\textsf{c}=1}^{\textsf{C}}W_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\boldsymbol{\tilde{Q}}^{t}_{\textsf{r}\textsf{c}}\,\left\{\frac{1}{L/\textsf{C}}\sum_{\ell\in\mathcal{L}_{c}}\left(\eta^{\prime}_{t,\textsf{c}}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}-\boldsymbol{h}_{\ell,\textsf{c}}^{t+1})\right)^{\top}\right\}

\displaystyle=-\left(\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}\right)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}A_{i\ell}\,\eta_{t,\textsf{c}(\ell)}(\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t}+\boldsymbol{V}_{\ell}^{t})\,-\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}_{i}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}-\left(\boldsymbol{Y}_{i}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{L}A_{i\ell}\,\boldsymbol{X}_{\ell}^{t+1}\,+\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}_{i}^{t+1}\right)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{Z}_{i}^{t+1},

(203)

where the second equality follows from the definition of
\boldsymbol{\tilde{Z}}^{t}
in (
91
) and the last equality follows from (
81
).

This completes the proof of the claims in (
179
) and (
180
), and Theorem
3
follows from Theorem
5
, the state evolution result for abstract AMP iterations. ∎

7
Conclusion and Future Work

This paper studied the Gaussian multiple access channel with random user activity in the many-user regime, where the number of users
L
scales linearly with the code length
n
.
Each user has their own codebook, and decoding may result in three types of errors:
missed detection
(
\mathtt{MD}
), false alarm (
\mathtt{FA}
), and active user error (
\mathtt{AUE}
). We derived two achievability bounds on the error probabilities (
p_{\mathrm{MD}},p_{\mathrm{FA}}
and
p_{\mathrm{AUE}}
), in terms of the user payload
\mu
and the distribution of the number of active users. The first bound is based on a finite-length analysis of Gaussian random codebooks with maximum-likelihood decoding. The second bound was obtained by characterizing the asymptotic performance of spatially coupled Gaussian codebooks with AMP decoding. In the special case where the users are always active, our asymptotic achievability bound is strictly tighter than the existing bounds by Kowshik
[
6
]
and Zadik et al.
[
4
]
for moderate or large user payloads (e.g., hundreds of bits).

We also proposed an efficient CDMA-type scheme with a spatially coupled signature matrix and AMP decoding. The AMP decoder can be tailored to take advantage of prior information on the users’ messages or activity patterns. Precise asymptotic guarantees on its error performance were derived by establishing a general state evolution result for spatially coupled AMP with matrix-valued iterates. Although we considered spatially coupled Gaussian signature matrices, using recent results on AMP universality
[
59
,
60
]
, the decoding algorithm and all the theoretical results remain valid for a much broader class of ‘generalized white noise’ matrices. This class includes spatially coupled matrices with sub-Gaussian entries, so the results apply to the popular setting of random binary-valued signature sequences
[
38
,
41
]
. Extending our CDMA-type scheme to handle other priors, such as modulation
[
7
]
, coding
[
35
]
, channel fading
[
13
]
, or common alarm messages
[
12
]
is a promising direction for future work. In particular, the AMP decoder with the thresholding denoiser (
100
) has low complexity and
could be integrated with a decoder for an outer code, similarly to
[
61
,
35
]
, to tackle coded random access.

This paper investigated random access in the conventional GMAC setting where users have distinct codebooks. It would be interesting to adapt the proposed coding schemes to
unsourced
random access
[
3
,
11
]
, where users share a common codebook and the decoder returns a list of messages without needing to identify the senders.
Several works
[
14
,
15
,
16
]
have investigated schemes for unsourced random access based on sparse regression codes (SPARCs) and AMP decoding. A coded CDMA-type scheme with AMP decoding could significantly reduce the computational and memory complexity compared to unsourced random access schemes based on SPARCs.

Appendix A
Implementation Details

In our experiments in Section
3.4
, we find that the asymptotic error bounds in Theorem
2
and Corollary
2

associated with
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
cannot be computed efficiently for user payloads
k>8
bits, while the error bounds associated with
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
can be computed in an efficient and numerically stable way for significantly larger payloads
k
. The exact threshold on
k
is determined by the precision level employed. For instance, with Python’s 64-bit (double) precision, we can handle up to
k<63
bits. This observation is consistent with those in
[
7
,
6
]
.

Since
M=2^{k}
, when computing the error bounds
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{MD}},\varepsilon_{\mathrm{FA}}
or
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
for larger
k
we may encounter numerical issues due to the terms with
M
or
(M-1)
in their exponents (see
(
64
)–(
66
) and (
76
)). To avoid such problems, we use the approximation

(1-\epsilon)^{n}\approx e^{-\epsilon n}
when
n
is large and
\epsilon
is small.

For all the experiments in Sections
3.4
, numerical integration is used to evaluate any expectation terms
\mathbb{E}_{z}[\cdot]
over the standard Gaussian
z
, including the expectation in the mutual information term in
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{marginal}}
(see (
57
)–(
58
)) or the expectation in
\varepsilon_{\mathrm{AUE}}
(see
(
66
)
or (
76
)). Monte Carlo sampling is used to estimate any expectation terms
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{z}}[\cdot]
over the multivariate standard Gaussian
\boldsymbol{z}
, such as the expectation in the mutual information term in
\mathcal{F}_{\textrm{Bayes}}
(see (
53
)–(
54
)).
